rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety. – mr president, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety (envi), most of the discussion we had was also on the sustainability criteria of bioenergy: for transport biofuels and also for the energy sector, when talking about gaseous and solid biomass. one clear point that the envi committee was pushing for was a total phase-out of palm oil use for biofuels. i am happy to see that in all the proposals, that part has been copied. so that seems to be a consensual issue within this house, and that is a good thing. however, the second point which was important for the envi committee was to make sure that the market will steer away from the food-based crops towards the advanced electricity. unfortunately, there are not enough proposals going in that direction: for example, the overall transport target that is still in the committee on industry, research and energy (itre) position, which will push for food based crops again – we will try to make sure that that 12% ambition is maintained, but not as an overall transport target but there where the ambition should go to advance. the third point on bioenergy for the energy industry: really exclude burning whole trees. that is the only thing we are asking and that seems to be the most sensible thing to do.
it is very good that i have just one and a half minutes to speak on one of the most important subjects of this mandate. because let's face it, energy is what keeps everything in our lives running: not just the economy, but our daily lives. but europe has three major energy problems: (1) the price; 2) it is still too polluting; 3) we are far too dependent on imports from outside europe. let 's be honest . 2017 was the year we imported more gas from russia than ever before. for all three problems, the solution is the same: energy savings and a huge input of renewable energy. that's the way forward! is europe aware of this? as sean kelly said: is europe really becoming the renewable capital of the world? not if we take the commission's proposal or the council's rather weak action so far as a starting point. i look at countries like china, but also the united states -- even under president trump: they're going much faster than europe. and india, too, has a much higher growth rate in this area than europe. it's unprecedented. so if we as europe want to move forward in this area, if we want to become energy independent, if we want to have lower energy prices and also if we want to stay within the paris climate targets, then more ambition is really needed. and that has been the alde group's line from the beginning. it is badly needed economically, ecologically and geopolitically.
mr president, we have heard some wonderful stories about clean energy today. but what they don't talk about is: the examples that the people who tell those stories give themselves. and those examples are important, because they're what make you credible. because sincerity is in the sacrifice. my question is: what sacrifices have the people in this room made to save their ideals, a good environment? i'm not talking about buying a toyota prius, which you don't have to pay taxes on. i'm not talking about free solar panels or subsidized led lights. that's easy. no, i'm talking about real sacrifice, stuff that hurts a little. for example, taking cold showers every day or not taking a plane to sunny places for a vacation. that sort of thing! if that does not happen, then the words here are gratuitous and unbelievable.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. i am thinking of the environment, innovation and european cooperation. but it is possible that these three points will be completely undermined tomorrow when we vote on mr mato's report. what do i mean? over the past decade, a very innovative and environmentally friendly fishing method has been developed in europe: the pulse fishery. proven benefits of this method: significantly less fuel consumption, significantly less co2 emissions, significantly less unwanted by-catches and significantly less disturbance of the north sea bottom. it is therefore logical that the european commission now wishes to authorize this method. but what do we see? misled by the paris-based loom organization, several delegates want to block this success story tomorrow. bye bye innovation, the environment and european cooperation. i would like to thank mr mato for his excellent cooperation. mr mato has shown himself to be a reliable european, a champion of the environment and innovation. his report deserves support! (the speaker answers a 'blue card' question in accordance with rule 162 (8) of the rules of procedure)
"blue card" is the answer. well, this method is fully in line with the european rules of the game. so it's not a game, and it's certainly not cricket! and we see the european commission now saying: let's allow this method. it's been extensively studied, it's proven benefits. so you're actually doing a very strange thing if you're blocking this beautiful innovative method. hence the wholehearted support for what the commission is now advocating and for the release of this method, of course within the existing quotas we have. that remains the framework within which we operate.
president. - mr president, i have to say that i am not in favour of the amendment. a race to the bottom of the sea, i understand. who can manage to empty the seas as quickly and efficiently as possible? the tree basket fishermen or the pulse basket fishermen? we know from the tree chorus that it has a devastating effect on the seabed and provides a lot of by-catch. we know from the electric pulse that it's deadly effective at catching tongues and in cod, it causes vertebral fractures and bleeding. but we don't know anything about the impact of the pulsar on the ecosystem. and we don't know what the effects are on sharks and rays, which are very sensitive to electricity. replacing one destructive fishing method with another is not the answer. let's put our emotions aside and wait for the scientific results before we lift the ban on the pulse chorus. we must also get rid of fishing gear that catches seabirds, dolphins and seals by-catch. a ban on driftnets is therefore necessary. i also believe that european agricultural and fisheries subsidies should be abolished.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem. an innovation that saves the seabed, the climate and the environment. this is evident from all the preliminary research results of wageningen university and wageningen marine research. and the commission took that as a basis. the dutch fishing families have invested heavily in this gear in recent years with european support and commitments to comply with the landing obligation. unfortunately, on the basis of fake news, a monster alliance and front of protectionism, the extreme right and the extreme left has formed in this parliament to put an end to this innovation. if their amendments are adopted, it will mean the death knell for many dutch fishing towns such as urk, den helder, scheveningen, stellendam and many more, and the death knell for the dutch whaling fleet. and above all - and do not forget this - the death knell for confidence in scientifically based and sound european legislation in this parliament. i call on all of you to support mato. because it was just said: some countries are going out of europe. and the netherlands, if we keep this up, too!
i welcome the european commission's proposal, which makes it possible to test new fishing techniques in practice. after a trial period and sufficient scientific research, it will be decided whether the new fishing technique can be used by our european fishermen: an admission procedure that is logical and accessible to everyone. i am therefore struck by the fact that some of my colleagues want to legislate in advance that pulse-cutting techniques should not be used by fishermen. the admission of the pulse chorus is thus politicised, rather than being decided by independent experts. what motivates them? is it an association they make with electric fishing? does the feeling of the gut play a part or is it purely for political-economic reasons and are the forerunners deliberately restrained? in any case, i think it is a shame that the pulse coral is not given the same opportunities as all the other new fishing techniques.
in 2015, there was a lot of pressure on the european parliament to agree to the adaptation of the common provisions regulation. exceptional measures were taken to assist greece in the form of additional prefinancing and a 100% cofinancing rate. it is therefore of great importance that the european commission communicates proactively and transparently on the effects of these measures. if this is not done, the effectiveness of these specific measures could be called into question. i think it is good that we have this debate, that we are starting to find answers to questions. but i still have questions, questions that remain. for example: what projects were finally 100% funded after the adjustment of the regulation and what was the effect on greece's competitiveness? i'm still in the dark about that, even today. i think it would be a good thing if the commission were to report to the european parliament on the effectiveness of these measures, in addition to this debate.
mr president, i would like to ask you why we are here. we're not here to get back at something we've loved doing: i'm going to be a very difficult situation. but the point is - the commissioner has just said this - that it is a very exceptional situation. we rushed her through here. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. and that's tax money! at the same time we agreed with the commission that it would inform us adequately. and when we appointed the permanent rapporteur, mr arimont, he asked questions. and actually the answer that the commissioner gives is great, but we want it on paper. we want to be able to judge it. so my question is very ultimate: i would like to see a debate on the four points we have mentioned here in the committee on regional development, in fact, and also with the others here in parliament. because i remember some of them not being so loyal to giving away that free money. so there was a lot of debate at the time, we used an accelerated procedure and you, as the commission, undertook to inform us and then to act. when will that be?

on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, when i was in kenya leading the eu election observation mission (eom), people would often ask whether we were holding kenya or africa to lower standards of democracy. the resounding answer is no. it is unacceptable to hold kenya, or any country for that matter, to a lower standard. instead, the eu assesses the extent to which elections meet kenya’s own legal standards and commitments under human rights and international law. unfortunately, during the 2017 elections, people’s rights were not always upheld and trust was eroded. our final report, which is available for everyone to read, looks at the gap between law and practice and gives 29 recommendations. the elections laid bare existing tensions and created new ones that all kenyans suffer from. no, the standoff has not resulted in as many casualties as in the past, but every death – and every death by a police bullet – is one too many. some kenyans complained that the world only has attention for the country when tensions escalate into violence. i believe the eu should be smarter and engage kenya on core topics that benefit all kenyans: strengthening the rule of law and good governance, supporting civil society and respect for human rights, fighting corruption and overcoming ethnic tensions. these are just a few areas that i do not think we hear enough about, while we hear lots about countering terrorism and trade and other such issues. anyone who has followed the discussions on kenya knows that over the past week strong words have been used against me. these statements have been revealing, but i hope mudslinging will not distract from the substantial discussion on our recommendations on electoral reform and on the joint way forward for kenyans. i would one voice. i call on kenya’s political leaders from both sides to show constructive leadership and rebuild trust. integrity should be baked into the electoral system, regardless of who is in charge and the independence of institutions such as the judiciary should clearly be respected by all. over the past days, when people read the more critical remarks in our report, they said: ‘but kenya is the most successful country in east africa’. yet, just because things could be worse, it does not mean that they are good or good enough. i remain convinced that we should never hold kenya to a lower standard, including on the respect for human rights. i have met many kenyans who are keen to enjoy the highest levels of democracy in their country. i wish them strength going forward and for the eu to be their principled partner.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on foreign affairs. – mr president, the billion-euro commercial market in ready-made surveillance systems remains largely unregulated, and that is astonishing in light of the capabilities that companies in surveillance, hacking and exfiltration technologies are further and further developing. while many politicians claim to be concerned with cyber security, anyone who can afford it can buy systems that collect massive amounts of data, can break into people’s devices without the consent of the user, and information can be removed unnoticed. this is unacceptable and regulation lags behind. the digital surveillance market should worry us in europe, but the consequences of exports to dictatorships where the rule of law is absent is even more grave and unacceptable. the arab uprisings seven years ago were often associated with the use of technology to advance human rights, but it has become crystal clear how repressive regimes have mastered technologies for their own goals and control, and this is not limited to the middle east. human rights defenders, journalists, critical citizens, dissidents and representatives of civil society are targets worldwide. it has taken a long time for there to be eu action but i’m very glad that we found broad consensus to update the dual-use regulation that will tackle this toxic trade with targeted measures on the basis of human security. surveillance systems will require a licence before export, human rights will become a clear criterion to assess before a licence is granted, and definitions will be clear, so the private sector will not suffer or be hindered unnecessarily, and we in turn count on their cooperation. similarly, we seek a predictable level playing field in europe. we need to end the notion of so-called ‘licence shopping’ where a company maybe rejected a licence in, for example, the netherlands, but can be granted one in, for example, italy. at the same time – and i am glad the commissioner mentioned it – security researchers should be able to do their job, which sometimes requires coordinated attacks on systems precisely to make them more resilient and robust. an encryption which actually helps people to protect data doesn’t belong on a controls list. we call for the lifting of export controls on that. i look forward to a swift trilogue that can start under the bulgarian presidency without delay. it is good to see such a coming-together of minds between our three institutions which can have a real impact in the real world.
i would also like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr buchner, who has worked hard to obtain broad support for this report. i am glad that we are now dealing with this report. it's really needed. the internet technology is advancing so fast that we are increasingly pursuing facts. there are several european companies that sell technology to countries with a bad human rights record. for example, we know that people have been tortured in bahrain based on text messages that they exchanged and that were intercepted with the technology of those european companies. we have to stop that. it is therefore important that there are clear and unambiguous rules which provide a basis for practical application. it is of the utmost importance that human rights are tested. otherwise, this review is really pointless. i would therefore like to call on my colleagues to support this report so that we have a broad base of support in the trialogue. i would also like to call on the member states to work hard towards an ambitious implementation. we're not there yet. and it's very important that we commit ourselves to human rights.
there are many fine words being spoken here, but the elephant in the room is that all the fine rules are non-deductible on the one hand and are being undermined by the arbitration clause on the other. mrs malmström, you have an obligation to ensure that companies have not only rights but also duties, and that they comply with them. the un committee on economic, social and cultural rights obliges states to include human rights in their trade policies. and while the oecd guidelines for multinationals are not binding, you can explicitly include them in free trade and investment agreements. the commission notes that the commission has not provided any information on the amount of the aid granted to the beneficiary. the european commission is very attentive to the wishes of companies and we know that investment protection is given full scope. how are you going to ensure that they move within the boundaries of the un and the oecd?
in writing. in my view, the cake is still unevenly distributed when it comes to the benefits of international trade. shareholders and employers benefit more from trade than, for example, workers or the environment. it's got to be different. not just for workers or the environment, but also to prevent unfair competition between companies. i admit that the history of the trade agreements concluded by the european union shows a positive learning curve. for example, the trade agreement with south korea includes a sustainability clause. however, this is not enforceable. in ceta we have called for the enforceability of decent labour and environmental standards. the european union must ensure that the chapters on labour and environmental standards can be enforced in practice. this means, for example, that people should be able to join trade unions and have the right to collective action. these standards must be enforceable at local level by people, with reference to the trade agreement. only in this way can the european union combat social or environmental dumping in third countries.
in writing. following the debate in the european parliament with bulgarian prime minister boyko borisov, the pvda's european delegation calls on bulgaria to push for a political agreement on the various migration dossiers as eu presidency. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment from harmful substances. in addition, it is important that bulgaria does more to tackle corruption in the country, as many other european citizens are also affected.

madam president, some years ago a number of adults – or should i say people pretending to be adults – created a global financial crisis because of their greed and immaturity. the crisis turned into an economic crisis which affected millions of young people. they had no part in causing it, yet the effects will affect their lives profoundly. this is why i think it is no more than normal that the eu member states have together committed themselves to protecting young people through the youth guarantee. it is not an issue of charity, but one of decency. luckily unemployment is decreasing now – and i am very glad that it is – but youth unemployment in member states, such as greece, spain and italy, which have already been mentioned by the commissioner, are still shocking, so we are not there yet. we owe it to young people to keep assisting them. firstly, we have to insist that jobs and traineeships made possible throughout our initiative hit the spot. this initiative should not only guarantee the development of skills among young people and ensure their integration on the labour market, because it is not only for jobs – the initiative is for life. i want to remind member states that it is not about lip service, but the future of their young people. secondly, i think the youth employment initiative needs to be transformed from an anti—crisis instrument into a more stable financing instrument for tackling youth unemployment. we need not only to maintain the initiative after 2020, but we need to increase the funding. may i remind you that the ilo fund, which guarantees young people a job or training, costs eur 45 million, whereas the current initiative has only eur 9 million to spend.
today we are discussing the implementation of the youth employment initiative. the european union has imposed an obligation on member states to offer a job, further training, a place in apprenticeship or a traineeship within a period of four months. an unrealistic ambition, where everyone knows in advance that such a thing is not possible. the eu's meddling and waste of money is again unlimited. once again, the impact of the billions invested is unmeasurable. the objectives are not being met and people are covering up in advance. and what is the solution of the world-alien european elite? with more money, of course. the taxpayer is paying for these absurd, unworkable plans. the report goes no further than opening doors and listing vague generalities. the only way to really tackle youth unemployment is to tailor it at the national level. in short, sovereignty and power back to the member states.
the first debate i was allowed to hold as a member of parliament in july 2014 was on this very subject. we then called at the beginning of this parliamentary term for youth unemployment to be made an absolute priority. looking back over the last three and a half years, i think we have succeeded in doing that, thanks in particular to the strong cooperation between parliament and commissioner thyssen. 132 measures have been adopted in the member states, 1.6 million young people have benefited from them and youth unemployment in the eu has fallen sharply. and yet i have a bit of a mixed feeling about it. because it's nice to be able to help so many young people. but at the same time, it's sad that this was necessary and still is. because we still see shockingly high levels of youth unemployment in many european countries. so more needs to be done, first of all in the member states themselves. structural reforms, better use of available funds and better matching of supply and demand in the labour market are absolutely necessary and can also be encouraged from europe. many of the issues have already been mentioned, but let me mention one specific theme: if we want to give young people a future in europe, we must not only want to cross borders, but also be able to see beyond them. i think one of the important things is: attention to neighborhood language education. member states should encourage pupils to learn the languages of their neighbours from a very early age. we shall be addressing this in the resolution tomorrow and i ask the commission to follow suit. in a beautiful border province like my own limburg, which is situated between germany and belgium, this is absolutely crucial.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, many thanks from my side to the commission for their strategy paper. if a few years ago, someone had told me that two vice-presidents would present a strategy on plastics in parliament i would have sent that person to the madhouse. so it is great to see that commitment from the commission, and leafing through the strategy, i must say the analysis is very strong, the language is right and the intentions are very, very promising. if the european commission were a consultancy firm i would certainly recommend it to all my friends and businesses around. but the commission is a political body; not only that, it is the only political body that can propose legislative proposals. and that is what is still lacking here. i know some elements are technically difficult: setting common standards for biodegradable plastics is not easy, and i know there’s a huge scientific debate behind that. but there are many other elements that are much less complicated and that have already been considered by the commission for years and years, even by previous commissioners. ms girling has already mentioned that tackling the use of micro plastics in cosmetics would have been easy, and it would have been quite easy to set minimum standards for recycled content in the ecodesign directive. and what about measures on single-use plastics? that has been done the around the world so concluding, i’m very happy with the strategy, but i had expected a little bit more on the legislative part. i know that your intention is to come forward with those, please do so during your mandate because if it is left to the next commission we don’t know if the same two vice-presidents will still be still there.

madam president, we, the socialists and democrats, have found new, unexpected allies and i don’t mean here in the chamber. i mean that, if the international monetary fund and the european central bank are pleading for a pay rise for employees in europe, what more need we say? yet can we stay silent on the consequences of rising social and economic inequalities in europe? let us start on a positive note. the european economy is growing, unemployment rates are falling and most economic theorists are telling us the fairytale that people will benefit from growth. but unfortunately not everybody is getting a fair share of the pie. on the one hand, we see companies still focusing on the well—being of their shareholders while the pay gap between the top chiefs and the average worker remains startling, and most multinationals are also still dodging taxes. on the other hand, we see the working-class families in europe falling behind. being in work reduces the risk of poverty. that is, at least, the normal theory. how is it possible then in europe that the number of working poor is still growing and growing? i will repeat what our unexpected allies are saying, namely that a pay rise would be good for business, for jobs, for workers and therefore for the european economy: a pay rise which, in my view, cannot be achieved without strong trade unions and strong collective bargaining. that does not, of course, help to reduce child poverty. we should address that issue separately. but a pay rise is really necessary.
the european union has for decades believed in the unfettered operation of the market. we all know that the market is looking out for the shareholders. we also all know that the market wants to keep wages as low as possible. this leads to flexible jobs and poverty. because with a job you can't make ends meet, your kid goes to school hungry. in the netherlands, the food banks cannot meet the demand of 135 000 people. in rotterdam, at the market, i see people looking for the last leftovers after closing time. that is europe. that's the netherlands right now. if we continue to believe in a market that we don't regulate, then the exploitation continues, the shareholders have a good time, and most people - the 90% who don't own two-thirds of the wealth of the other 10% - are screwed. i want a social protocol that subjects the market to social rights and not a non-binding social pillar.

madam president, in recent months, mr juncker, mr tusk and mr barnier have sounded more and more like the spice girls – please, uk, tell us what you want, what you really, really want – because it has been 627 days since the referendum and we have mostly heard from the uk about what they do not want. somehow, a very narrow referendum result has led to a blueprint for a hard brexit, where the uk cannot stay in the single market, cannot stay in the customs union, cannot have a customs union and cannot have a norway- or a canada-style deal. ms may wants to take back control of laws, money and borders, but at the same time, she speaks of the most comprehensive trade deal ever with frictionless trade and no border in ireland. where is the sense of reality? her speech last week was an opportunity, after two years, to provide and to paint a realistic picture. instead ms may again limited herself to empty slogans, carefully drafted to hold together a divided cabinet and a divided tory party, still desperately trying to present brexit as a win-win situation while asking for many more cakes to have and eat and many more cherries to pick. i am glad that this house today states that that is a completely unacceptable position. it is time for ms may to stop confusing the brexit negotiations with conservative party management and to start developing a credible and serious negotiating position. let’s get on with that.
mr president, we often talk about brexit as a technical fact, but i want to emphasize once again: it's about people and the consequences for those same people. after ttip, we agreed that trade agreements should never lead to a dilution of eu norms and standards. so the future trade agreement with the british is also a matter for consideration. so, good enforcement of food safety and environmental standards, for example. we also want guarantees that the uk will not develop into a trading partner that undermines our social standards and encourages fiscal dumping. otherwise, it is the european workers who will be the victims of their political decisions. in ceta we agreed to seek higher standards through regulatory cooperation. only if the british also go along with this can it lead to a lasting and fruitful trade relationship.
madam president, time is running out. the uk government is encouraged and, actually, urged to make and articulate choices on the future trade relations. political promises cannot remain vague and contradictory forever. for us, a level playing field is essential. we should not make concessions when it comes to high standards or compromises on the unity of our single market, but it is time for a reality check in london: special trade privileges are only negotiable when the united kingdom also accepts overarching rules and standards. yet the uk’s many red lines only lead to one thing: there will be hurdles and friction when it comes to trade, and that negatively impacts citizens, small and medium-sized enterprises and, of course, many other companies. a solid future relation does not magically emerge on the basis of speeches. i therefore urge prime minister may to come up with a realistic proposal about the trade relations she does want for her country after brexit in relation to the european union.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) on the conclusion of the agreement in the form of an exchange of letters between the european economic community and the kingdom of norway on trade in textile products (com (97) 0406 - c4-0173/97). that pillar is slowly starting to take shape, but we need to pick up the pace. the commission's proposal for a european labour inspectorate is being presented today, but the social security number announced earlier has already been dropped. the european labour inspectorate is a step in the right direction, a step towards a decent and fair european labour market, by tackling exploitation and unfair competition between workers and unfair competition between companies at european level. the proposal also gives europe teeth to bite when it comes to cross-border crimes. but, mr president, if the heads of government really want a social europe, they must act now. we want to see action, not just words.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, trade rules and negotiations are complex. we saw this during years of negotiating the transatlantic trade and investment partnership (ttip), negotiations that are stalled after a dramatic change in the american approach. normally trade arrangements are certainly not the kind of topic to be shaped in a number of angry tweets one morning – tweets that sent stock markets plummeting and gary cohn resigning. we condemn the harsh accusations in the eu’s direction and frankly are puzzled by what we see. adding insult to injury, the president’s tweets now also speculate about imposing tariffs on european cars and other accusations. for a country that exports so many german cars, this is at least a bad business proposition. many jobs are created in the us by european companies and vice versa. we don’t live in a zero-sum world but we are actually mutually dependent. senior officials have suggested national security is the reason to impose broad steel and aluminium tariffs also in europe, and as allies we believe these arguments are misguided and not justified. the same goes for the notion that individual member states may negotiate exemptions. american hints at undermining the eu as well as british hints at being open to special treatment can only be read as being made in bad faith and must be challenged, where the british governments will find out soon, if necessary, what the benefits of a common approach globally in the eu are (or were, in their case). for decades the us and the eu have worked together to craft a rules-based system: rules for human rights, war and peace, and also trade. does that mean that the system or the world trade organisation (wto) is perfect? rapporteur. – madam president, it is good to be here. this week, the financial times reported that corporate tax rates have been falling ever further since the crisis, and it raises the question of sovereignty. the only sovereignty the member states currently have is to lower the tax rate, whereas i would think that if countries had a choice, they could not just decrease, but also increase the tax rates. the proposal that the european parliament discusses today is a real game-changer. it changes the nature of tax competition. let’s start with consolidation. consolidation is the principle that firms no longer decide where to make profits and pay taxes, but it is up to the countries to decide where they make profits and where they pay taxes. it puts an end to profit-shifting. this reduces the pressure towards lower tax rates within the eu. but we also need a profit border control, and that has been included in a proposal by the european parliament as well. controlled foreign corporation (cfc) rules and switch—over rules – it sounds a bit technical but what it amounts to is very simple: if a tax rate in a country outside eu is lower than 50% the member states have the right to tax extra, according to the statutory rights. this is a border control that we need to have in place. reducing the pressure on marginal tax rates and making sure that we let multinationals pay their fair share. that’s what people ask from us. the second important element in the proposal is taxing the digital economy, which has long been overdue and should have been done much earlier. we have made calculations that google and facebook didn’t pay 5 billion in taxes over a three-year period, because we are working with an outdated corporate tax system. that is a problem. the current corporate tax systems are outdated. they come from a time with chimneys and blue-collar workers. but globalisation and digitalisation has changed things. and location has become a choice, something that is particularly true for the tech giants, which have no physical presence but are very present in our lives. and in this way, they avoid paying taxes in large, high-tariff countries and reduce their tax bill. third, and i think this is a very clear statement from the european parliament, we think that the current proposal amounts to a simple and robust corporate tax system. it should not just apply to the large corporates, but should be extended to include all firms. so we need a lower threshold to give benefits and bring a level playing field to all the firms within europe. i hope very much that the european parliament will tomorrow give a very clear signal that, despite all the political differences we have, we can agree that we want a fair, efficient and modern corporate tax system for the 21st century. that could be the signal for the countries to start debating this. i very much hope that especially the large countries, germany and france, will put pressure on the smaller countries, countries like the netherlands, ireland, malta, luxembourg that are the pirates within the european union. they come to another country and try to appropriate these countries’ tax base. these are not the actions of a european partner. a fair and efficient tax system in the 21st century is what we need. i call on the support of the european parliament but i also call on the countries to restore their sovereignty and back up these proposals in this year.
rapporteur. – madam president, i would first of all like to thank my colleagues for their contributions in this debate. special thanks also go to the rapporteurs and the shadow rapporteurs for working together on these files, as well as to alain lamassoure and also to pierre moscovici. i think we worked together very productively. i very much appreciate it. we have the vote tomorrow, but it doesn’t mean our work ends after tomorrow. we can’t just have a vote in the european parliament. we still have to reach out to national parliaments, national governments and the public to put pressure on for this reform. i also expect that from members of the european parliament, and i am sure that alain lamassoure and i will continue to work outside this house to press for this reform, because this year will be the moment of truth. i am very pleased when pierre moscovici says that this year we should have the discussion in the council and this year we should put the pressure on. this is the year when the member states have to show their true colours. in the end, i think that, even if we don’t have the 27 on board, we should push this, because it puts pressure on the countries that remain outside an agreement. currently, the situation is that google and facebook are in dublin, but the tax reasons for being there become much less if other countries already start to tax google and facebook. this will change the dynamics. hearing this debate, another point came to my mind: first of all that reform is very difficult, especially for countries on their own. they fear losing competition, so the best option for reform is to do it together. so we need to restore sovereignty for member states as well, by trying to reform together. i hope, again, that parliament will give the starting signal and vote in favour with broad support for a fair and efficient corporate tax system in the 21st century.
mr president, i want to talk about two things tonight. first, i want the commission to investigate cases where companies decide to relocate their production within the eu. we need to make sure that eu funds have not been used in such a way that they are actually creating competition between member states. secondly, it is unacceptable that large companies can pretend to be socially responsible by developing social initiatives, or through charity, when, on the other hand, they are not paying the taxes they owe to society. i heard the commissioner mention a roadmap, but can i please ask for an ambitious new action plan on corporate social responsibility (csr) with legal requirements, with stronger reporting obligations and with more effective control mechanisms? to come back to the question of taxes, this action plan should also include the disclosure of tax payments in csr reporting. it is time to expose this hypocrisy, and for that i am counting on the commission and its proposal before the end of the year.
the economic sun is shining in europe, but for many europeans it is still a bright light. youth unemployment is still high in a number of member states and many older people are marginalised in the labour market. the europass is therefore a good complement to help people get back to work: a free online platform where people can easily create a cv or add information to an official certificate, making it easier to understand for employers or institutions abroad. in an era of increasing cross-border work, increasing online job search and the ability to send applications digitally, a platform like europass is a logical choice. we must nevertheless ensure that people are not disconnected from the european labour market. digibetes are at risk of losing their connection and this group deserves our continued attention. nevertheless, i thank the rapporteurs for their work. the proposal for a reformed europass at the speed of the people is of course supported by me.
the european union has a duty to ensure that the rights of the palestinian people are respected. but i'm happy with some marginal notes. one of them is: i did not want to restrict imports of raw materials. i also wanted to regulate consumer goods, like our inseparable cell phone. it didn't work. the other thing i regret is that it has to take so long. america already imposed import restrictions in 2013 and we're not actually going to check until 2021. i'm sorry to hear that. but anyway, we got it. and now we have to make sure that it's there in 2021 and that it's implemented properly. my questions to the commissioner are therefore: how do we make sure that the development money that's available for that implementation is not just going to companies to get their bureaucracy in order, but that mining communities in countries like the congo and their governments are given the space to actually control those companies? the next question is this: what have we done ourselves, what has the european commission done to change the procurement rules so that we too do not buy computers here without knowing what minerals and metals they contain, and do you also make such demands on service contracts from commercial suppliers? i'd like to know. one last point, if i may. there is support for regulations to restrict trade in human rights violations. textiles, for example. please do something about that, because the next step has to be another product.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on energy, research and technology, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) on the common organization of the market in electricity and hydrogen. and to meet the paris climate targets, we have to switch to alternative fuels. this includes an ambitious policy for the roll-out of the infrastructure for these fuels. action at european level is crucial. i see the action plan as a first step. let's put the consumer first. the consumer with an electric car must not stop travelling across europe. information on the availability of charging stations shall be reliable. information on costs should be available and transparent. and the payment, when you load, when you use fuel, should be easy. i call on the member states, i call on the commission, i call on industry to take much more ambitious steps to develop infrastructure for these alternative fuels. because only if we put industry, the commission, the member states and consumers at the centre of this together will we really make sustainable transport a european success story.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, over the years, ugandans and partners have expressed outrage at cases of child sacrifice, essentially perpetrated by witch doctors for certain rituals. yet, there is an equally alarming practice referred to as ‘mercy killing’. this implies parents of children with disabilities killing their children or deliberately letting them die of starvation or through the denial of medical attention. poverty and social stigma play a significant role, but not much information is available on the scale of the problem or the number of children concerned. in a recent documentary by a local tv station, some parents confessed to having engaged in mercy killings to save their children from severe suffering throughout their lives. some also confessed to acts of mercy killing to save themselves from the difficulties of raising a child with a disability. although uganda’s legal framework provides for the protection of children, and specifically that of children with disabilities, only limited initiatives have been taken to avert the practice. this is partly attributed to the apparent social tolerance of such killings. the situation is further complicated by the fact that people with disabilities often face marginalisation and rejection. such entrenched social barriers, coupled with inadequate support from communities and government, as well as families, can leave carers with the feeling of having no choice. the european union, through the european instrument for democracy and human rights and other initiatives for the justice and law and order sector, provides support to the government and to non-state bodies to address issues affecting children and discrimination against marginalised women and children. we ask the government to address the problem of mercy killings. parents of children with disabilities need support in providing special care for these children. the government is encouraged to put in place adequate social services, medical care, special needs education and other facilities to enable children with disabilities to enjoy the same rights as any other children. the government should, furthermore, ensure that the perpetrators of mercy killings are brought to justice. the eu stands ready to work with the government of uganda in addressing this issue.

rapporteur. – mr president, let me start by seriously thanking my colleagues for the wonderful spirit in which we worked in the last month and for more than a year in order to come to an agreement much time we still have left with current co2 emissions in order to stay within the paris agreement. we all so strongly supported the paris agreement and, to refresh your memories, the paris agreement also tries to stay within the 1.5 degrees scenario. for the 1.5 degrees scenario, the time we have left in the medium estimate – so that is not the most extreme – with current co2 emissions is four months and 22 days. four months and 22 days! so it is obviously clear that we need an extreme sense of urgency to stay within that scenario, and it’s clear that during the negotiations with the council, the member states have not shown that sense of urgency. on the contrary, it was extremely difficult, extremely difficult, to get the council moving, and even the arguments that we used from parliament’s side, that with the energy efficiency, a proposal from the commission of 30% instead of the 27%, and with the upcoming facilitative dialogue in the un process, it was absolutely necessary the coming one or two years, they wanted to stay on the lower side by concluding this act of law, which is quite scandalous. so if you ask me if i am satisfied with the final result we agreed, i’m afraid my answer is no, but it was the maximum that we could get out of the council and i think it would have been irresponsible if we had not tried to find an agreement there. but still, i am not satisfied. nevertheless parliament did achieve quite some improvements in this climate action regulation because now we finally got rid of that awful name of ‘effort sharing regulation’, from now on we can call it clearly the climate action regulation. parliament fought hard to get an additional 125 million tonnes of ambition and i am proud of that, and we did tighten the trajectory. member states cannot sit back during the first few years when it kicks in from 2020 onwards. and, very important, there is a strong reference to the long-term climate strategy that the eu and the individual member states must develop, and the european council recently confirmed that by asking the commission to come forward with proposals. so we might now finally get rid of the october 2014 conclusions. we also came to a strict review clause, and that one is extremely important, also in light of what the commission can do in the coming year with the talanoa dialogue and the long-term strategy. i am convinced that this is only the start and that with increased stringency in the coming years we might tighten it up.

rapporteur. – madam president, in less than two weeks the commission will publish its proposal for the multiannual financial framework, and a lot of attention will go to the eu budget after brexit. but we should not forget about the future of the eu’s foreign policy spending, with almost eur 100 million budgeted for external relations already, a lot is at stake, and we must use this upcoming budget as an opportunity to reflect and reform in order to make the eu a stronger global player. it is undeniable that the current foreign policy spending structure needs improvement. evaluations, independent ones, have found that a strategic and overarching vision is lacking, while management by different services within both the commission and the european external action service are inefficient and incoherent. information sharing with us here in parliament has been anything but forthcoming and, similarly, legal barriers have prevented the eu from swiftly responding to new and emerging challenges. we cannot make opaque trust funds the new model for foreign policy spending and certainly not with the main goal of managing migration, and that is exactly what risks happening. the european external action service is rumoured to seek the creation of one single instrument for eu external action with ‘a strong focus on migration’. you heard it right: the goal is to pool all the money together, and as we understand, commissioner hahn is trying to ensure the funding intended for candidate member states and the european neighbourhood remain separate. what’s most concerning to me and many of my colleagues is not that there is an appetite for ambitious reforms – in fact that should be embraced – but that no governance model has been set up for such a single instrument. so how can we in the european parliament be asked to agree with the new vehicle if we do not know how it is going to be steered? i am afraid that the lack of clarity here uncovers a bigger problem. during a recent committee on foreign affairs hearing and during the many foreign instruments working groups that i have chaired, it has become clear that members of the european parliament are not in the mood to give the external action service or the commission carte blanche. they feel sidelined already as strategic dialogues are often neither strategic nor dialogues. information is shared reluctantly and late. parliament is keen to ensure it can do its job and steer policies, spending and apply the necessary and required parliamentary scrutiny. meps also want to build in more flexibility to ensure adequate responses to new crises, as well as the preservation of european values. we do, as europe faces unprecedented challenges at the moment: bloodshed continues in yemen and syria, and presidents erdoğan and el-sisi have further consolidated their authoritarian grip on power in turkey and egypt. at the same time, governments in russia, china and iran are at odds with the values and interests we hold dear in europe. in practice, the values-based goals and objectives of eu foreign policy have too often become subordinate to short-term and national interests such as migration management or countering terrorism. the deal concluded with president erdoğan to stop syrian refugees from reaching us in europe is a case in point. the future of the eu’s foreign policy budget can benefit from reforms, but cannot only be designed to deal with today’s crises. who knows what could be at the top of the agenda in 2025? and in the absence of an altogether more flexible budget, which is what we should be focusing on with the mff, we need at least to build in flexibility while we have multiannual spending plans. but certainly the instrumentalising of all foreign policy funds and managing migration as the key goal is not the right direction. so before the european external action service and the commission propose a new architecture, they should think about governance and, where needed, be reminded that we in parliament play an equal role here. i therefore encourage colleagues in this house to focus on the principles of parliamentary scrutiny, oversight, policy-steered spending, evaluations and accountability. let us not get bogged down in long lists of our favourite instruments to save from the reforms.
rapporteur. – madam president, i would especially like to thank all the colleagues who broadly support the compromise in the resolution. in replying after the debate, we have one key question left that can be summarised in one word, and that is ‘how’. we hear, on the one hand, that the mid-term review concludes that the instruments are fit—for—purpose. on the other hand, we get a proposal to radically change the instruments. i think the commission and the external action service really urgently need to explain how – and not only to us (we are budget and foreign policy geeks, not a big group of people, although we would have been interested in an actual outline and we have asked for it before), but what about our citizens and what about our partner countries? this cannot wait until the last minute, if you really want this to be a success. i think, generally, the notion of better communication has been raised by meps throughout many discussions we have had. so i unfortunately have to conclude that there is still no clarity on governance. it is a broad ask and, frankly, i think it is a missed opportunity that this debate was not used to outline how the reform of the instruments will be governed. on a number of focus areas that you outlined in your introduction, such as public diplomacy, the western balkans and a digital values—based approach, i think we generally have common ground, but i am concerned when i hear things like ‘we will work according to what the people want’. of course, this is a highly political discussion and i suspect it will be for the entire multiannual period that we are talking about, and i urge the external action service and the commission not to lose sight of long-term goals and objectives: even though short-term solutions can be very tempting, they can also be a populist trap. i really think that we, the eu, have a bigger promise than only to look at short-term goals. certainly when everything gets framed in the context of migration management, i do not think that that is a long-term and a sustainable and values-based solution. i know a lot of colleagues are very unhappy if that is the direction we are going in. to conclude: yes, our money should be focused on policy goals, and should be spent efficiently with the appropriate oversight and parliamentary scrutiny, while respecting our values. we can only really talk about whether we agree with the new architecture if we know how it is going to be governed.
president. - the commission considers that this agreement with australia is in the interests of the people and of the world. but is it also in the interests of animals? like the eu, australia is sending live animals to the middle east and asia. last week we saw shocking images of thousands of sheep succumbing to heat on board such a large australian sheep ship, and every night thousands of kangaroos are hunted down and killed in australia. baby kangaroos are kicked to death and left helpless after their mother is murdered. europe is a major importer of kangaroo meat and is therefore partly responsible for these problems. in addition, the eu is failing to protect its citizens from kangaroo meat contaminated with salmonella and e. coli. article 19 of that agreement deals with animal welfare. i ask the commission to put its word to its deed and, in the interests of both man and animal, to come forward as soon as possible with a ban on the import of australian kangaroo meat. i also believe that european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, thanks to mark zuckerberg, the general data protection regulation (gdpr) is now the best known european law ever, even in the us congress. yes, thank you, mark, this one’s for you! i think mr zuckerberg would be well advised to appear before this house out of respect for hundreds of millions of european facebook users who feel deceived. there are actually more users here than in the us, and i think zuckerberg should be here. we should not be visiting him in the united states. he should be here. but today’s debate is not about facebook. the european parliament, yes, and also the european data protection authorities will scrutinise very closely whether the new facebook privacy arrangements are compliant with all eu legislation, but we also need to take a very critical look at ourselves. we talk the talk, but do we also walk the walk, commission, council and dear friends? because amongst those who are voicing their outrage at facebook are many who have consistently voted against stricter privacy protection, and continue to do so. like those – including the president of this house, dear friends – who summoned mr zuckerberg to the european parliament, but they voted against e-privacy, so a bit of consistency, please. i also note that the european data protection authorities have hardly responded to earlier reports on cambridge analytica and facebook. it has been known since last year – why have they been so slow? and despite the jubilation about gdpr, member states have not nearly allocated the necessary means to the national data protection authorities to enable them to properly enforce the gdpr. i also note that the advice of designated experts, like the european data protection supervisor and working party 29, are being systematically ignored. their advice on data retention, pnr, safe harbour: ignored! and all subsequently scrapped by the european court of justice. worryingly, decisions by the european court of justice are also being ignored. now will the commission remain deaf to the recommendations of working party 29 on pnr and privacy shield, or act? because while we are busy barricading the front door to keep the likes of mr zuckerberg out, the back door is wide open. and we are giving away our way our personal data by the truckload, not just to our own government agencies, but also to other countries like the united states, with legal safeguards that carry about as much weight as donald’s tweets. european citizens are left out in the cold, unprotected. yes, the facebook case is a wake-up call for all of us. mr zuckerberg, privacy is not dead. it’s alive and kicking.
mr president, i was allowed to read a very large advertisement in my newspaper. it said: "new eu legislation means better data protection for you" and i thought: what a hypocrite you are, mark zuckerberg. you, who spent years lobbying with your big money against better european data protection laws. you who claims that what you do is bring people together, that it's not making money. but it is. it's about making money. it's about making money from our data, and it's time that we make laws to ensure that mark zuckerberg has a different business model in the future. i am indeed glad that there is now new european data protection legislation, but we don't owe facebook for that, it is the last thing we owe facebook for.
mr president, the line between online and offline is blurring, so we need to be extra vigilant about the use of personal data. facebook has rightly come under fire because a cambridge analytica questionnaire collected personal data. but not only the fillers, who had given their consent, but also their facebook friends, had their data taken away and they didn't know anything. in the netherlands, it was revealed last week that healthcare companies are using a facebook pixel to share sensitive information with facebook. that is why i am pleased that new european legislation will come into force next month. in this case, personal data can only be used with the consent of the individuals, and then enforcement by european bodies and by the dutch is crucial. i also believe that the commission should put an end to its arrogant disdain for this parliament and re-open the appointment procedure for a new secretary-general.
mr president, facebook is suddenly a fan of european data protection rules. the wonders are not yet over. but it did require a major scandal where data from millions of european users was shamefully passed to a shadowy company, cambridge analytica, and used in political campaigns, without those people's consent and without their knowledge. facebook knew about it, but kept quiet for two years. that's not really surprising, because i jokingly took facebook's reaction to those new rules from europe about six years ago and they said very clearly: we should have focused on encouraging best practices by companies like facebook rather than on setting out detailed rules that may be frustrating and costly. overly prescriptive provisions on consent have an adverse effect on user experience. an overly prescriptive nature of regulation on data-based notification creates a level of bureaucracy that only distracts. that's facebook as we know it. not too much fuss, not too many rules, make a lot of money. but now mark zuckerberg has apologized and had a beautiful ad placed in european newspapers advertising those same rules. and now we have to believe him on his blue eyes. i don't believe it. i know. this knee-jerk is too little, too late. mark zuckerberg has to answer to the eu, to this house, and i expect the european regulators to apply the new rules that facebook is so big on, and apply them to that company from day one, very hard.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). we see nothing at all in such plans. first of all, i think of the european commission's plans as involving meddling, pointless and unnecessary arrangements and interference with national sovereignty. we are firmly in favour of the free market economy. companies are responsible for their own success and should not be hindered by european bureaucrats who have no idea what healthy competition and market functioning actually mean. we are against the transfer of powers to the european union. we are against the waste of european taxpayers' money in supporting unprofitable companies and bankrupt shipyards. in other sectors too, it has been shown in the past that state aid and subsidies merely delay execution. it's often nothing more than a temporary cover for the bleeding. the airline industry and the car industry, for example, are two examples. the netherlands has also learned from the damage and the shame of state aid. in the 1980s the dutch taxpayer paid billions and billions of guilders to support shipyards in our own country, ultimately to no avail. large shipyards were closed after a few years and tens of thousands of jobs were lost. so all the tax money had been for nothing. in recent years the dutch shipping industry has been flourishing again. not because of the government, but because of the power of entrepreneurship and innovation in the sector itself. in the end, it is the market, not government, that determines where and when what goods are produced. it's a question of supply and demand, price and quality. in short, stop the so-called aid programmes, stop the european subsidy infusion, curb the regulatory drive and leave the shipping industry alone!
the european commission has today presented the long-awaited financial version of the new multiannual budget. to our astonishment, the budget is increasing by as much as eur 300 billion, whereas it should have been reduced by some eur 100 billion just because of brexit. the netherlands is also ignoring the annual rebate of 1 billion euros under the guise of simplification! this budget means that the netherlands will now have to pay 10 billion euros a year instead of the current 7 billion euros. the dutch taxpayer will lose €1,000 a year to the eu if it is left to the commission. i propose to cut back and cut back on expenditure by, among other things, stopping subsidies, aid programmes and financial support to countries like turkey, cutting the salaries and allowances of officials and parliamentarians and, of course, stopping the strasbourg circus. this is the time to make adjustments and to cut the budget considerably. the netherlands should not pay more but much less to the failed eu project.

president, investment in transport infrastructure under cohesion policy is producing concrete and visible results. transport projects help to reduce the developmental backwardness of parts of europe. we have had the opportunity to witness this with a delegation from the committee on regional development, for example in sofia, where the european union is investing in the metro. but investment in infrastructure - i want to emphasise this - also strengthens the internal market. we often say in the netherlands: without transport, everything is at a standstill. mr novakov's report rightly outlines the challenges for transport infrastructure beyond 2020. completion of the trans-european transport network, improving the sustainability of transport, and applying innovations. these are all very important challenges. to this end, after 2020, we need to make complementary use of the connecting europe facility, the cohesion fund, the european regional development fund, as well as funding from the european investment bank. i also call for more resources from the european regional development fund, specifically for crossborder investment projects in sustainable transport infrastructure in europe. the interreg programmes are a perfect tool for this. in this way, with the resources of the european regional development fund, we can invest in the missing links in the infrastructure of our border regions. and then, mr president, we can really begin to connect the people of europe.
