the north sea is one of the most complex fishing grounds in the world. we are dealing with almost 100 different species, and fleets from eight member states. i am in favour of regionalisation in fisheries, because the different fisheries in the european union have different species and conditions. so you have to take those differences into account, and we're doing that in the new policy. it started with the baltic sea plan: that was the basis for this north sea plan, and that basis is good. that is why we wholeheartedly support the north sea plan as it stands. we also have great appreciation for the approach and the work of mr rodust. we can do that now because this plan is continuing the development of a more successful north sea policy. all the major fish stocks in that sea are at their peak. there is one other concern, mr president, and that concerns the hearing we held last week in the committee on fisheries. then we saw that there are still many questions about the landing duties. we must resolve these issues together, otherwise untenable situations may arise.
the north sea is a beautiful natural area. it is the largest nature reserve in the netherlands, and it is home to a great variety of species that deserve protection. unfortunately, the north sea is not protected as such and this plan does not give the north sea and the animals that live there the protection they deserve. in fact, protection measures for sharks and rays have been removed entirely from the original proposal and a trick is being used to allow overfishing to continue even after 2020. this is contrary to the common fisheries policy and it is contrary to the development objectives of the united nations. mr president, we as a parliament cannot accept that. in september last year a large majority of parliament voted to stop overfishing. the member states refused to discuss this. now it is the turn of parliament to keep up. we do not authorize overfishing. i also believe that european agricultural and fisheries subsidies should be abolished.
on behalf of the group of the european right mr president, i think it is very important that we regulate the criteria for access to the european union for fishery products, because the european union has a large market for fishery and aquaculture products. the figures have already been mentioned: with the import of these products, we represent a quarter of the total global market and we are over 60% dependent on imported seafood for our consumption. that is why i support the engström report, because the honourable member stresses that imported fishery products must meet the same requirements as products from the european union. we must create a level playing field and this report is an important step towards a balance between eu and non-eu producers. as far as labelling is concerned, we really must do better than that. so far only a small group of products have been labelled, whereas we should be properly informing consumers about all fishery and aquaculture products. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the fisheries sector is not left behind. well, we are doing a good job of it and that is why we are also supporting the report by mr aguilera.
thank you, chairman. i would like to see a debate here in the european parliament, commissioner, on what we would like to see in the long term in agricultural policy, and not go into details. what i do not want, for example, is that the agricultural budget is still necessary to provide a certain income for the farmer and that the agricultural budget is spent on subsidizing land ownership. i would like to see farmers standing on their own two feet, keeping their own trousers on, and the money from the agricultural budget actually being invested in the business of the sector to meet the future in this changing world. i want us to be able to deal with price fluctuations, to deal with crop failures, but also to have the sector provide solutions to combat climate change, to reduce environmental impact, to preserve biodiversity and landscape elements. the world around us is changing. the farmers want to move forward. now the european parliament.
the average age in the agricultural sector is high, above average, and only 6% of those working in the agricultural sector are under 35. however, lowering the average age is not an end in itself. i am concerned that the agricultural sector will still be attractive for people to work in. we are not going to solve this by giving young farmers more direct income support, direct income support paid for by cutting the direct income support of the other farmers. is that fair? older farmers also work hard every day to produce sufficient and good food. no, it can only do so by making the sector more competitive, more innovative and by ensuring that the sector comes up with solutions to climate and environmental problems. i think there are two ways of doing that. through technical solutions such as big data, solar panels, precision agriculture and ecological innovations such as soil health improvement, breeding, and the use of insects to control pests.
mr president, we have a very successful programme here tonight. the so-called cef programme covers not only transport but also energy and telecommunications. i think we have proved in the last period that this is one of the best investment programmes the european union has ever known. this is not a question of subsidies or transfer expenditure, but of sound investment in the future of the european union itself. this debate tonight, mr president, is intended to give commissioner bulc the final push to keep that cef programme going and to extend what my colleague mr marinescu said as far as possible. this is really about the future of the eu and it is more than worthwhile to have a debate on it at this late hour tonight.
president, 180 billion a year is needed for the transition to a sustainable economy. yeah, that's a lot of money, but that money is there, just in the wrong place. one million is provided by banks as loans to the fossil fuel industry, apart from the many billions that go to the arms industry, land grabbing and other practices. it has to be sustainable. that's not the case with quarterly capitalism, where only the next quarter's profit matters and banks and other financial institutions don't look beyond their nose. it must be different and it can be different, by radically improving the rules of the game, by rewarding good behaviour and punishing bad behaviour and by giving clear responsibility to traders, investors and banks. i therefore hope that the commission and the member states will take up the recommendations contained in the report. we'll have that 180 billion together, and we'll avoid having the 2008 crisis in a completely different way. finally, i would like to thank the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteurs for the good cooperation and for this excellent report.
president, the world around us is changing and it is therefore good that the european union is investing in new priorities, such as the protection of external borders, tackling or mitigating climate change, ensuring greater security, cyber security. but as with any budget: when you're making it, you have to make choices. so if you need more money for something, you'll have to cut back somewhere else. i think it's very important that we start looking critically at the old priorities, that we start reforming them, and that we can free up money to start investing in the new priorities again. what i also think is important is that we look at how the money is being spent and whether it actually produces results. i think it is a missed opportunity that these two aspects are totally ignored in this resolution of the european parliament. the cuts proposed by the european commission are being reversed. everything must be left as normal as possible, and extra money is foolishly asked for. that may be the easiest way, but that is not my policy and i will not support it.
rapporteur. – madam president, after 27 months of hard work we are here today to vote on the revision of the posting of workers directive, and therefore we are voting on a very important value for europe, that all workers deserve equal treatment and that there are no first and second class workers in europe anymore. up to this day, we see colleagues on construction sites, in agriculture, in shipyards, and many more sectors, being turned into competitors, not because they want to be, but because some are less expensive for their employers than others. with this new posting directive this will change. we will put an end to both the undercutting of wages and the inhumane situations we sometimes see when it comes to lodging, and we can put an end to unfair competition between those companies that play by the rules and those who bend the rules to their own benefit. therefore, i am grateful for the courage and the political will of so many, because otherwise we would not have ended up here today. first of all, i want to mention commissioner thyssen. i think she feels in her bones that the situation that workers are currently facing is unfair. secondly, i would like to thank all ministers of the member states who really aimed at reaching a fair compromise during the concluding negotiations under the bulgarian presidency, and if you allow me, i would like to single out my colleague and former dutch social affairs minister lodewijk asscher. but without the good cooperation of elisabeth morin-chartier, without the shadow rapporteurs, without the committee on employment and social affairs, without all the meps involved, and of course without all the staff, we would not have made these steps towards a fairer european labour market. it is only together that we can curb the race to the bottom when it comes to wages, working conditions and social security systems. undercutting wages and unfair competition leads to resentment from citizens, and this has to stop. i think the revision is beneficial not only to all european workers and to all honest companies, but it is also good for the european project as a whole. the freedom of movement of workers should become a right of workers again, and with this new directive we accomplish this. workers’ rights are explicitly protected. so instead of being competitors, colleagues can become colleagues again when today we vote in favour. we can ensure a fairer labour market and we can turn the slogan ‘equal pay for equal work at the same workplace’ into reality. with this step i think we are also taking a big leap towards a social europe, to which we are committed under the social pillar. today it is up to us to put an end to the exploitation of posted workers and to ensure equal pay for equal work at the same workplace for all.
i want a fair european labour market. a labour market where people who cross the border are not exploited, but are given what they are entitled to. a labour market where the fraudulent companies that abuse the freedom of movement through sham structures and letterbox companies are also dealt with. and a job market where we end unfair competition and displacement. to do that, we need two things: strict, fair rules and good enforcement. in the area of these strict and fair rules, we are taking a very important step today, because for the first time we are clearly establishing in legislation that the provision of a service is always temporary and always of short duration, that collective agreements apply to all employees and that rotational posting is unacceptable. there is still a lot of work to be done, but the legislation we are adopting here today is a very good step towards a genuine, fair european labour market.
- i'm the reporter. mr president, i think it is worth emphasizing once again that the free movement of workers is also a workers' right. workers have the right to seek an economic future if they cannot find it directly in their own environment. but workers also have the right to equal treatment and not to be exploited. workers have the right to work in companies which are actually willing to play by the rules. and if i am happy about this proposal, it is partly because we have stated at the outset of the proposal that the rules are also intended to protect workers. it is about the free market and also about the protection of workers. i think we have tried to take account of all the different interests and to put forward a balanced proposal. there are people who have said in the debate: there are other problems in the labour market. yes, that's true, there are more problems in the labor market. but there are other proposals which are being discussed here in parliament. i'm calling the social security coordination. i call the european labour authority. i'm referring to the discussion of the employment contract. we have a lot on our plate until the next election. but first things first, let's send an important signal with this vote: equal pay for equal work at the same place of work. europe has no first- or second-class workers. everyone deserves equal treatment.

in a letter, a brussels square full of empty shoes 4 500 in total symbolized the number of victims in the gaza strip since 2008 on monday afternoon. the whole world is looking at israel and palestine again, including the european foreign ministers who met that day to discuss the heightened tensions. protests were expected in the run-up to the 70th anniversary of the state of israel. and without compromising the long history and complexity of the problem, the disproportionate violence on the israeli side is unheard of. shooting at demonstrators who do not pose an acute threat is unacceptable and further encourages escalation. an independent investigation into the violence is desirable. both sides have an obligation to their citizens to (return) to the negotiating table, because only a two-state solution can end this decades-long conflict. that means putting an end to settlement policy, inciting violence and refraining from unilateral action.
mr president, commissioner and colleagues, at the moment we most need to strengthen rules-based trade in the multilateral system, our friends across the atlantic are breaking with american and transatlantic trends, making the us an unpredictable partner, and this plays into the hands of those who are seeking governance and trade models contrary to those of liberal democracies. so where we would ideally cooperate to address steel dumping onto the global market, the trump administration has used the illegitimate argument of national security to slap tariffs on european steel and aluminium. it has pulled section 232 from the cold war era at a time that no one should long to return to. not only does the invoking of this section hurt our shared ability to solve problems, it also hurts the transatlantic relation. i share the commissioner’s assessment that these us measures are against wto rules. we need to push for permanent exemption and support you in imposing rebalancing measures if the eu is forced to do so. what is certainly not appropriate is to pressure the eu into negotiations with the us on broader trade arrangements and to defrost ttip under these circumstances. sure there’s a lot we can talk about, but not with these tariffs hanging over our heads or with a metaphorically loaded gun pointed at us. this is the moment for strong, united european leadership – that means including all member states – leadership towards trade that benefits all, and of course, this means standing against unfair trade practices, and i’m glad to hear some colleagues in this house who used to want to blow up the trade rules themselves now standing here to defend them. so there might be a little bit of a silver lining in all of this very, very troubling situation.
the european parliament, in its report on schengen, is in favour of open borders and therefore against security. like the european commission, parliament does not want to hear criticism of illegal border crossings and is blind to their criminal consequences. even after 1,200 attacks in one night, parliament has not opened its eyes. the arrival of huge numbers of illegal migrants has led to the reintroduction of border controls, including in germany, and rightly so. even mrs merkel - the ace of multiculturalism - seems to be shocked by the rapes and the crime, and border controls have not been abolished. if even merkel wants fewer illegals, how is it that this european parliament is blind to the misery that the open borders have brought and shows no compassion for the women affected?
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, after dramatic changes in global developments as well as trade flows, it is high time our trade defence instruments are reformed to be fit for purpose. as christofer fjellner said, it has been a long time coming. we, in europe, remain open for business and committed to rules-based trade without being naive or defenceless vis—a—vis blatant violations that cheat our citizens. we need one method to assess substantial market distortions, whoever may be suspected of engaging in such unfair practices. of course, we must be wto compliant and lead by example in the trade measures that we apply ourselves, and i’m glad we have positioned the eu properly by this modernisation. this house has focused on the transparency, predictability and efficiency of the new rules, and has zoomed in on the particular needs of small and medium-sized enterprises. yes, we have considered importers and exporters and not just listened to the voices that shouted the loudest. it’s essential that europe leads in promoting rules-based trade globally, ensuring that trade rules and trade flows benefit everyone – again, without being naive. this package fits well with the discussion about screening foreign direct investments; it’s really the other side of trade defence instruments. we need protection without protectionism, and i think we show how it can be done.

blue-card answer. – i think, as rapporteur on turkey, having drafted a report every year, which your group supported with a large majority, i think it’s clear that we are concerned about the situation in turkey. but i just want to make clear that we have here an oral question asking the commission about investigating its responsibility about how eu-financed money was spent in turkey. the conclusion could be that actually we are implicitly co—responsible for very serious human rights violations in the country. i just hope that in this debate we don’t turn it into a general debate on turkey, but we ask the commission to answer specifically the various serious allegations that are in the media and reported by human rights organisations.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, firstly, we are here today to discuss the eu pre-accession funding to turkey and how it was used. so i am interested to learn from the commission how much money the eu has, from 2014 until today, provided to turkey, not just under ipa ii, but basically broadly during those years. this would provide important context to the amount of money we spent. secondly, we requested this debate because it was reported that the turkish military has used eu-funded cobra vehicles to shoot at and forcefully push syrian refugees back across the border. now, if confirmed, eu funds would have been illegally used and the eu could be indirectly complicit in human rights violations. so has an independent investigation into these allegations been undertaken and, if confirmed, what will be the consequences? would turkey only have to pay back the money or would parts of the accession funding to turkey be suspended as a result? in other words, what kind of accountability mechanisms are there? because what we know is that the commission has often been hesitant to use conditionality. it took amendments from us in this house to the 2017 budget to make sure that funds to turkey were decreased as a direct response to the significant backsliding of the rule of law and democracy. freezing and redirecting funds should be a proper policy option. what is also clear is that there is a lack of transparency, monitoring and clarity on objectives when we look at funding. no one seems to know exactly how much money is spent, where, why and to what effect. the court of auditors – and our colleague mentioned this – drew similar conclusions in its may report and i am actually still waiting for answers to my written questions about this report. the commissioner is proposing new plans for eu pre-accession funding on 14 june as part of the broader budget package and of course we are happy in this house to push for making eu spending more transparent, accountable and effective. so we need to learn how funds will be governed and how parliament can apply its scrutiny. we certainly will not want to see a repetition of the statements on migration and the hastily assembled trust funds over which this scrutiny is lacking.
mr president, i am the fourteenth speaker. at the beginning the commissioner made a contribution saying that this debate was actually based on a misunderstanding. i still wonder why this misunderstanding has not been cleared up when the european commission is financing turkey to buy two cobra vehicles, a kind of light tank which can also be very easily fitted with a rocket launcher. by the way, those cobras are made by a turkish defense company, so we're funding a turkish defense company for turkish light tanks that can be equipped with rocket launchers. then what's the misunderstanding? was there a misunderstanding in 2011 when we did that, or was it a misunderstanding when the launchers were actually put up there? if we know that turkey is shooting refugees at the border and pushing them back into syria, then i do think that we have a huge misunderstanding here. but it's not just him in those tanks.
in writing. recently, the story of kenyan nice leng'ete went viral in the media. she was able to end the ritual of female circumcision in the masai community in kenya and tanzania. however, these and other practices of female domination are still widespread in the world. the european union also has a lot to do in terms of gender equality, such as closing the pay gap. nevertheless, we're doing quite well on gender equality worldwide. but instead of sitting back, we must not forget the rights of women and girls in other parts of the world. violence against women must stop. they should not be married off as children to an unchosen man. they must be given the opportunity to occupy the same positions as men. only then can they develop fully and use their talents. it is therefore of the utmost importance that we continue to actively promote women's rights both within and outside the union.
mr president, the future belongs to the young. it is a proverb we know in almost all european countries. but is that saying still relevant today? youth unemployment is still alarmingly high in many european countries. the number of young people living in poverty or social exclusion has increased. in the united kingdom, a whole generation has been denied a european future against their will. that is why the debate tonight is so important. that is why i am pleased that the european commission has proposed to double the budget for the erasmus programme. it is very good that young people are a priority and that we are focusing on all young people. it is also right that we in this parliament should say that crossborder cooperation and crossborder opportunities are an essential part of youth policy. i myself come from a beautiful border region and i am sure that there are also many opportunities for young people there. opportunities that are still too often missed. we must ensure that work and skills are better integrated and that we dare to look beyond borders. because whereas a young unemployed person may have difficulty finding work in his own country, there are always opportunities on the other side of the border. we should want to learn our neighborhood languages again. we need to work even better and more closely together in border regions and we need to ensure that young people have access to information, funding and professional guidance. this is a task for young people themselves, for schools, for guidance counsellors, for local and national authorities, but certainly also for us here in europe.
mr. president, there's no guarantee you'll be able to pay your rent tomorrow. no guarantee that you'll be able to work tomorrow and uncertainty about accident insurance at work. nobody wants uncertainty, and yet there are employers - in my country, the netherlands, for example - who are increasingly addicted to flexible working. on the one hand we see the money mountains of multinationals like mcdonald's and uber and their shareholders growing, and on the other hand they are not paying into the collective benefits and saddling their staff with insecure contracts. and with that, the deliveroo of this world will become the dagobert ducks of europe. work no longer seems to be a guarantee against poverty, and therefore the economic burden must be distributed differently and more fairly. increase taxes on capital, raise wages, as the imf also recommends, and finally put an end to uncertain contracts, because uncertain jobs eat people.
author. – mr president, while human rights in iran are systematically violated, european leaders have most attention for the rescue of the jcpoa. preserving the nuclear deal is important, but we must be able to address human rights as well as iran’s toxic role in the middle east. dual nationals are used by iran’s rulers as political pawns. they suffer in prisons without access to lawyers, doctors and eu diplomats. often they are forced into confessions, after which torture and possibly the death penalty can follow. the fact that eu citizens are seen as only iranians when they still have that nationality tells us all we need to know about the countless iranians and how they are treated unjustly in prisons. we call for their release, and we appeal to high representative / vice-president mogherini and member state leaders not to allow talks on the nuclear agreement to overshadow our responsibility to address the grave human rights violations in the islamic republic of iran.
author. – mr president, despite charm offensives by mohammed bin salman, saudi arabia is showing its true colours by arresting prominent women’s rights activists, right when the driving ban that these women fought for is promised to be lifted. then, state-linked media labelled these women as traitors, and they have since been charged with serious crimes. this sends a chilling signal to all human rights defenders in the kingdom and beyond. it is also a wake-up call for those who still needed it. it will be unsustainable to repress the population and women in such a systematic way, as happens in saudi arabia every day. the promise of reforms under the 2030 strategy has been tarnished, and we should hold saudi arabia to its acts, not just its beautiful promises made alongside celebrities in foreign countries. the release of women’s rights activists should be the first step and would be a first important step, as we urge, to grant basic rights to all people in saudi arabia. we will not be impressed with some small gestures.
president. - we are discussing today the abuses committed by aid organizations such as oxfam. in recent months, stories have emerged of perverted sex parties in haiti. oxfam staff have behaved scandalously there, at the expense of the european taxpayer, among others. since 2010, hundreds of millions of euros have been sent to haiti. it is only since the beginning of this year that we have begun to get a picture of how that money has been spent, and that picture is not pretty, i can tell you. the scandal in haiti is not, unfortunately, isolated. for example, some oxfam staff who committed misconduct in haiti were found to have committed the same acts in chad a few years earlier. it's outrageous and unacceptable. oxfam is certainly no exception, for other organisations have also been the subject of scandals. take greenpeace, for example. two days ago, it was announced that as many as 44 former staff members from south america had dropped out of the school. several instances of undesirable behaviour and abuse are alleged to have occurred in recent years. the complainants were fired, the perpetrators were held to account. it's a disgrace. the list of examples of abuse is getting longer and longer. almost every charity has to deal with it. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. but when it comes down to it, everything stays the same. each year, europe spends over 75 billion euros on development aid and related issues. of this, 10 billion will be given away by the european parliament. mr president, it is time to stop the shameless transfer of billions, the transfer of billions to corrupt dictatorial regimes and so-called aid organizations. this is despite the fact that there is little or no control of these expenditures and that the public has no idea what the money is being spent on. this has got to stop. development aid and all related expenditure should once again be the exclusive responsibility of the member states. each country must be able to decide for itself whether and, if so, how much money is to be transferred to the bottomless pit called development aid. mr president, as far as i and my party are concerned, the message is clear: we are not going to give a penny to corrupt dictatorial regimes and so-called aid organizations. people who have misbehaved should be dealt with harshly by the criminal justice system.
mr president, a good, fair and reliable pension system is one of the pillars of our modern welfare state. this goes beyond a government that simply provides benefits after retirement age. this means that citizens themselves are investing in a good income in their old age. the responsibility of the public authorities is to ensure that as much of the money earmarked for pensions as possible actually ends up there. it is not right to leave unnecessary money on the administrative line without providing an efficient solution. i therefore sincerely regret that the rapporteur, together with the negotiating team, has chosen to give the pension funds and the clearing houses only two years to find a solution to the clearing obligation. because it takes more time to do it right. failure to take that time will unfortunately lead to a good, sound and fair pension provision being compromised. i am therefore glad that this is only a parliamentary position: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i hope and indeed expect that the trialogue negotiations on this legislation will finally correct the mistake made by this parliament. thank you, sir.
on behalf of the ppe group mr president, i thank the rapporteur and the commissioner for their introductory remarks. but i think a special word of thanks today goes to my party colleague marian marinescu. this was a very sensitive issue and he has brought several difficult discussions with the shadow reporters to a successful conclusion. the safety of air transport is one of the most important subjects that the european parliament can discuss here today. on 17 may i myself visited easa in cologne with mr marinescu and other members of the european parliament. i am impressed by the agency's current mission and even more so by the tasks it will have in the future. easa also has a very good international reputation. it is not just the eu that is at stake, but also relations with the usa, china and south america on aviation safety. i think that we as the european parliament can be proud to have such an agency. now there's an extension of duty. it will not only be about aircraft and certification, but also about drones and soon also about the equipment at airports. these are all subjects that make aviation safety complete. the interesting question, of course, is: "is the united kingdom staying or not?" the uk cannot, of course, continue to be the only country to have aviation safety. so we'll have to see how things develop. thank you very much.
the european parliament has called on the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the safety of passengers is not compromised. the commission is also proposing to introduce new european rules on the use and registration of drones, new opportunities to use the specific knowledge of aviation inspectors across europe, and new tasks for the european aviation safety agency (easa) to intervene when aviation safety is threatened. but new rules, new opportunities and new tasks require cooperation between easa and different authorities in the european member states. i am therefore pleased that a european system is being set up to warn european airlines of dangerous routes. disasters like that of flight mh17 could thus be prevented in the future. but this requires a good working relationship between national intelligence services, aviation authorities, the european commission and easa. intelligence services will collect and share information about risks of flying over conflict zones. joint risk analyses shall be established to enable easa to provide timely warning of hazards to the european aviation industry. that, mr president, requires mutual trust and cooperation. this requires the conversion of paper rules into workable solutions. i therefore call on the member states to work together with easa and the european commission in a united and strong way. tomorrow we will put the new easa regulation to the vote, but safety calls for action today and every day to come. because the safety of passengers cannot be compromised. i never will.
thank you very much, mr president. in the netherlands we have a wonderful saying: "without transport, everything is at a standstill". road transport in the european union is a major economic factor. our economy is still very much based on road transport. if you look at the discussions on the mobility package this week, you know that road transport is very important for the european economy. but we all signed up to cop21 in december 2015, and we want the transport sector to be cleaner too. it shouldn't take too long. i agree with mrs bulc on that point. at the same time, clean trucks also bring innovation. it's not so bad that politics is giving a little push here and there to accelerate that innovation. in that respect, i would like to ask for a moment's attention to the city. if i understand correctly, our buses are also covered by these heavy vehicles, and bus pollution is really a very big issue in various cities. finally, mr president, i am glad that the trialogue also resolved the debate on data and industrial property. i think that european industry was right to fear that too much access to data could lead to industrial theft. i understand that was settled in the trialogue.
thank you, mr president. thank you, chief. the european commission is being pushed hard. and the commission is pulling us forward at full speed, while italy is trying to pull us back in time. the proposals to create new european pools or to pay off debts directly to the neighbours have been launched at a rapid pace. and sometimes it seems like this house only has two gears: either full ahead or hard on the brakes. but as far as i'm concerned, there's a third. and that's the option where we hold ourselves to the agreements that we've made with each other. that's the option where we make our economies resilient, and we remain a major economic bloc of the world. that is the option, as far as i am concerned, where we do not send each other the bill, but where we trade with each other. making appointments is actually very easy. but only if we hold ourselves to those agreements, then, in my view, is there room to share those risks in confidence. that's the order as far as i'm concerned: we must make agreements, we must keep our agreements, we must sow trust and reap prosperity.

thank you, mr president. education is a matter for the member states and must remain so. but there is still a world to be won in terms of cooperation between member states in this area. and it is local initiatives that can make the difference. if i look in my own region: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area, such as exchange projects between dutch and welsh schools, teachers who work temporarily in a german school to learn the language, crossborder internship projects and so on. that is the added value of cross-border education. and we do not have to arrange that in brussels, but we can facilitate and encourage it and i am glad that this house is endorsing it. there is a clear european challenge, for example, when it comes to children of travelling parents such as carnival entrepreneurs or circus performers. we must ensure that these people have access to high-quality education for their children directly via the computer, wherever they are in europe. there are already a number of member states where this is a possibility. but please let us make this possible for all the children of europe.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, after the g7, president trump left not a millimetre of doubt about his opposition to his main allies. anyone who was not yet convinced now knows the eu needs foreign and security autonomy. our strength is tested in the process of keeping the spirit of the joint comprehensive plan of action (jcpoa) alive. the eu has chosen, with international partners, to bind and verify iran’s nuclear programme through the international atomic energy agency (iaea), and our part of the deal is lifting sanctions. of course, we are committed to peace, diplomatic solutions and, first and foremost, that which we ourselves have signed up for. so to have this policy autonomy is key. certainly, to have the us choose to abandon one of the biggest diplomatic successes at times where these are few and with so much at stake is enough for us to absorb, and so we simply cannot accept that washington spells out which sanctions our european companies are bound by. i would be interested in what is possible to mitigate us extra territoriality without pushing private-sector risks into public institutions. at the same time, our autonomy must be clear in tehran. iranian leaders cannot and must not dictate what we in europe should do. iran itself must build confidence that they are committed to their part of the deal if they expect us to try and work hard to absorb the challenge of the us stepping back. i’m interested to learn a bit more about how conversations with china and russia are going, because the spotlight is so much on us. in previous debates, i have made clear that i believe that we have lost too much time focusing only on iran’s nuclear programme and not on the country’s toxic role in the middle east as well as the systematic human rights violations against the iranian people. i urge you to make sure the eu has a broad agenda in relation to iran and not to be trapped in the single issue of saving the deal, however important that may be.

on behalf of the enf group. – mr president, i welcome mr rutte to this parliament. i guess we will see him quite often in the future when he has accepted the position of mr tusk as president of the european council. when we discuss the future of the eu, we most certainly must address the situation of the rule of law in the member states. the majority of this parliament has serious concerns about the rule of law in hungary, poland, malta and slovakia. therefore it has started the monitoring group on the rule of law. your party is a member of the alde group, and a colleague of yours in this parliament, ms in’t veld, presides over this monitoring group. i advise this monitoring group to take the netherlands as a reference case. in the netherlands, the prosecution doesn’t have the obligation to follow up on any reported offence. it is at the discretion of the prosecutor’s office whether to take action. therefore, millions of citizens in the netherlands see prosecution as a political tool. this was recently confirmed in a report by wodc, a scientific institution that advises the dutch government. the report stated that over the last decade there has been no prosecution of extreme—left violence. i will give you another example. last month, a group of rejected asylum seekers looking for a place to stay broke into a house near amsterdam, claimed the property as theirs and tried to chase away the inhabitants. although this attempt failed, the state prosecution didn’t want to take legal action, and they allowed this behaviour. to me it’s absolutely clear that applying the law to one group and letting another off the hook creates legal inequality. it breaks the rule of law. these are the netherlands of mr rutte and ms in’t veld of today. i have some serious advice for mr rutte with regard to his european ambitions. if you want an eu where the rule of law applies, start at home. and i quote: ‘get your house in order’, and have the prosecution take action against illegal activities in general, not just against so-called populists. a second issue i would like to address is illegal migration. the eu is in a complete deadlock on how to deal with illegal migrants. the eu has no solutions. it doesn’t close its borders; it doesn’t expel rejected asylum seekers; it doesn’t fight islamisation and radicalisation. so i urge mr rutte to distance himself from ms merkel’s ‘refugees welcome’ policies and endorse the visegrad approach. he could start by making it a criminal offence to help illegal migrants, rejecting asylum to migrants who enter via safe third countries, and making asylum seekers who go on holiday in their home country lose their right to stay. when you are president of the european council, recommend the whole package that we rapporteur. – madam president, this is the second part of mobility package i. after the posting of ms merja kyllönen, we will have now driving and rest times for the drivers. to be honest, i have to fight today against fake news. in this proposal, the actual driving and rest times of the drivers are not being changed. everybody is giving us information that we are changing the driving and rest times, which is fake news. we are improving the labour and working circumstances and conditions of the drivers, and with our proposal a driver has to go home after the second week – so he goes home in the third week. if you look at what is happening now with nomadic drivers, who stay away from home for seven or eight weeks, this is really an improvement in the drivers’ labour circumstances. we will also give drivers the opportunity to spend their weekly and their daily rest times in proper parking places where there are toilets, the possibility to take a shower and possibilities to eat. this is not a small detail, this is the truth. and in addition to this, the enforcement of this regulation by the smart tachograph will be improved in 2024.

the rapporteur. - mr president, when you talk about eu transport policy, you are talking about the example of the internal market. we in the european parliament want the free movement of goods and people without too much administrative burden. but we also want a social policy and a social policy which at least supports and helps the drivers. but as mrs kyllönen said, we have a mobile market and the mobile market requires more flexibility. i would like to reiterate that the epp group believes that the package proposed by the committee on transport, which combines the three issues (namely posting, cabotage and driving and rest periods) in a balanced way, is a balanced package. but, mr. chairman, we are not blind. we have, of course, seen the vote in june as a defeat and we must move. the empl committee also has a clear view of what is going on and what needs to be done. in my report on driving and rest periods, we have adopted a number of amendments from empl which will hopefully bring the matter into balance and lead to a positive vote tomorrow. i have reduced the reference period from four weeks to two weeks because we want to give drivers a decent and balanced rest. the commission has already made a number of proposals. i have to tell you frankly that i find that many people do not understand what is said in my report on those parking spaces. we respect the judgment of the european court of justice: i don't usually rest on freeways in bad parking lots. you may only spend your rest, including your weekends, in good parking spaces with all the necessary safety and sanitary measures. so sleeping in the parking lots is the exception, not the rule. it might be good in this age of fake news to just repeat that. i would like to appeal in particular to my colleagues in the s&d group, who supported an earlier compromise on this point, which i myself supported, and who now seem to be backing down. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. persons are not containers and containers are not persons. we have made a distinction on a number of points so that the two streams can function together in a decent, but also social, way. finally, mr president, i would like to say this: if we cannot reach an agreement tomorrow, the liberalization of the market will continue unchanged. we are already seeing the necessary transport companies from ukraine, belarus and turkey moving into the european market without any form of social protection, without any form of internal market regulation. and we shouldn't want that.
president, it was in this chamber that jean-claude juncker announced three and a half years ago that he was going to pursue a triple a social europe, and many people were in favour. a social europe does not need first- and second-class workers. we therefore do not want drivers in europe to be treated as second-class workers. it is clear in these proposals that transit transport is excluded from the rules on secondment and we are prepared to talk about the danish model. so there is room for compromise. this is not the case with regard to driving and rest periods, because truck and bus drivers also have the right to adequate rest. to be on the road for weeks and hardly taking breaks is exhausting, it is bad for health and it is bad for the safety of everyone on the road. that is why we have put the empl proposals back on the table and i call on you to vote for them.
the mobility package, mr president, unfortunately reveals new fault lines in europe, fault lines between eastern and western europe, between countries on the periphery and those at the centre. but, mr president, is it not the role of this european parliament to bridge those fault lines? a united parliament can send a strong signal to a divided council. of course, the compromise as it was worked out in the committee on transport has imperfections. but the beauty of the compromise is the balance between the internal market and social working conditions for all european drivers. international hauliers and drivers are getting the benefits of the single market, but not the administrative burdens. we're also ending the unwanted nomadic existence of drivers and we're starting a smart tachograph. president, the title of the mobility package is 'europe on the move'. let us make a move on this matter by supporting the mandate of the committee on transport and negotiating. let's move on.
substitute author. mr president, helping people in need is a moral duty for all of us. if a danish motorist sees a syrian family with small children choking on the side of the highway, it is only human to give them a lift. when spanish firefighters on greek islands save people from drowning, they deserve our applause. nevertheless, the danish motorists and the spanish firemen were prosecuted for human smuggling. the 2002 directive, with its very long title 'on the definition of assistance in the event of illegal entry, illegal transit and illegal residence', provides that each member state must have appropriate sanctions in place if a european citizen helps someone to enter or move irregularly in that member state, if this is contrary to national law. the directive also states that if a european citizen is seeking personal financial gain, that member state must prosecute that citizen. because that's a crime. but, and i prefer to speak of irregular rather than illegal, if a european simply provides assistance to a migrant without a right of residence, that cannot be criminalised under the united nations protocol against smuggling. the (brief) 'definition of aid' directive provides for this in article 1 (2) with a non-binding option for an exception for humanitarian aid. but that exception has not been understood correctly in all cases, otherwise those danish motorists and those spanish firemen would never have been prosecuted. the european parliament therefore asks the european commission to formulate clear guidelines for the member states on the application of this directive, so that the exception is regulated and that the provision of humanitarian aid is subsequently incorporated in each member state either in law or in practice, and we make it clear once and for all which forms of aid should not be made punishable in member states. i think it is of the utmost importance to emphasize that at this time. the ship is waiting in malta for the seawatch to be cleared to sail. the aquarius is in marseille, in the harbour, because it was no longer allowed to stock up in italy. there are hardly any ngo rescue ships operating in the mediterranean at present. and that's when the law in italy is very clear: humanitarian aid is not a criminal offence. in austria, belgium, germany, the united kingdom, finland, sweden, the netherlands, luxembourg, portugal, croatia, ireland - and this list is not exhaustive - there is also a distinction made in those countries between people who smuggle for financial gain, which is a criminal offence, and people who help out of humanitarian motives. that distinction is very important. in france, the law has recently been tightened, making it more difficult for aid workers to provide aid. whereas in hungary, since the vote on the new ngo law, it has actually become almost impossible to help migrants with simple things like food and drink and a place to sleep. so while many countries distinguish between aid and exploitation at home, they seem to have lost sight of this in mediterranean rescue operations. the council conclusions of last week state that all vessels operating in the mediterranean must respect the laws in force and must not interfere with the work of the libyan coastguard. of course, all ships at sea must obey the law. why does that need to be stressed? don't these ngos follow the law? i think you do. i think they respect the law and also the law of the sea in which one principle is very clear, namely that a drowning person must be saved. why is there such an emphasis on the operation of a third country's coast guard? the un protocol against smuggling, which clearly states that the provision of humanitarian aid is not a criminal offence, does not apply to the mediterranean sea? and the european directive on the definition of assistance? so, mr president, it is very desirable that the european commission should provide clarity to the member states on the interpretation of the directive. thank you, sir.

eight years since 2010 that we can now look to the future with more optimism, that we can now talk about the definitive end of the economic crisis and adjustment programmes. let me say, dear and dear members, that i am happy and pleased that my government is the one that succeeded where three consecutive governments from 2010 to 2015 failed. this in itself is a remarkable political success. but what makes me really happy today is not that we have succeeded in completing the adjustment programmes. because this could be achieved by some other political force following a recipe that would leave the great social majority vulnerable, would leave them unprotected, as happened from 2010 to 2015. if i feel that we have achieved anything, it is that on the one hand we have succeeded in deepening the structural reforms which have led us to fiscal consolidation and to the exit from the crisis, while at the same time we have succeeded in protecting, as far as we could within the budgetary margin we had, the great majority of the population. this was the great difficulty and the great success of these three years: the fact, that is, that in 2015, when i came here, greece was on the verge of a great humanitarian crisis, because it had two million uninsured citizens who had no right to access health care; because there was no possibility for 600 000 people of the guaranteed minimum income, the social solidarity income; because there were real people who had lost not only their income, but their jobs, their future, their present and they still had not - still had not to meet their daily needs, still - still had not to eat and they were in the soup kitchens of social organizations by the thousands. this image does not exist in greece today. no, i don't want to be here in front of you telling you that it's a "success story". but it is a path that, step by step, paves the way to the future. and if there's one thing i really want to tell you that i'm happy about, it's that we've managed to deepen structural reforms, to set a course for fiscal consolidation, to achieve primary surpluses that no other government has been able to achieve with much, much tougher fiscal adjustment measures, while at the same time we've managed to protect the most vulnerable, not just the uninsured, not just the poor, but most importantly to set a precedent for protecting workers' rights. commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the budgetary discipline is maintained. with growth rates of 0.3%, which achieved 0.4% just one year in 2014? how would he manage to achieve that pace that would bring back 20% of gdp? well, these are targeted inaccuracies. it is also incorrect to say that greece spent 40 billion on recapitalising its banks in the same three years. the total cost of all three recapitalisations was 45 billion, but the recapitalisation in our own years cost only 6 billion of the 24 available from the third budgetary adjustment programme. i say this because there are some things that need to be answered. and i say this because i believe that if we are to look ahead, we must, with our existing ideological and political differences, at least, when we dispute, dispute over the actual numbers and the reality that we have all faced. i want to close, ladies and gentlemen, by saying this: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this great and real danger, which concerns the very future of the european union - an existential danger for europe - is not, as i understand it from many of the interventions, treated as such. i would like to say that we are facing this great danger, if not primarily because in recent years we have indeed prioritized policies of extreme character, which were of course aimed at economic equilibrium but, in order to achieve it, tried to save the banks without taking account of the social costs. it is these policies that give voice today to extreme political forces, with a leading role. i want to address once again the entire democratic arc, from the left of social democracy to the liberal center, but also to you, the people's party, because these forces are within you and they are becoming dominant, hegemonic in political discourse. when you are forced to turn your political rhetoric towards nationalist, extreme, chauvinist positions in order to reverse or stop the rise of the far right in your countries, you are doing nothing more than feeding these extreme forces which will soon reach rates that will be worrying for all of us in europe. and that must be stopped! to stop it we must return to the founding values of the european union, which are solidarity, which is social cohesion, which is the well-being of citizens.

rapporteur. – mr president, let me start again and let me welcome prime minister orbán, who i wanted to shake hands with, but he turned up late for the debate. (murmurs of disapproval)today, we are debating the state of the rule of law, democracy and fundamental rights in hungary. tomorrow, president juncker will present his state of the union, and i asked you before – i ask you now again, colleagues – today, what is the state of our union? what shape are we in? article 2 of the treaty on european union reads: ‘the union is founded on the values of respect for human dignity, freedom, democracy, equality, the rule of law and respect for human rights, including the rights of persons belonging to a minority’. it continues: ‘these values are common to the member states in a society in which pluralism, non—discrimination, tolerance, justice, solidarity and equality between women and men prevail’. by signing up to the treaty on european union, all the member states made a commitment – an obligation to defend, respect and promote these values, regardless of political differences. are all the member states fulfilling these commitments? i’m afraid not. the hungarian government has effectively silenced independent media. it has put academia on a leash, and future generations will not learn to appreciate critical thinking. it has replaced independent judges with those with a closer tie to the regime. it has rules on which churches are allowed to worship and which churches are not. it makes life miserable for ngos that provide services to citizens in need, such as homeless people, migrants and refugees and marginalised groups like roma. these are services that – to add insult to injury – lighten the work of local governments and are often financed through european union funding. on top of that, individuals in the government have enriched themselves, their family members and their friends by means of public funding from european taxpayers’ money. the report that is before you today comprehensively lists the actions that, together, represent a clear risk of a serious breach of the values of our union. unfortunately, nothing has improved since this report was voted on in june. on the contrary, this summer, one of the last independent news channels changed ownership and, from one day to another, became the mouthpiece of the government. the propaganda tax on ngos got through parliament, and whether the central european university can continue to function in budapest is still a big question. since 2010, the european parliament has been urging the commission and the member states to act. they did not do so. and it’s particularly disappointing that the member states turned a blind eye to the structural erosion of the rule of law in a fellow member state. it is not the commission alone that is the so—called ‘guardian of the treaties’. we are all the guardians of the treaties. (applause)we all have the task of protecting the rights of european citizens to live today in a society, as article 2 reads, ‘in which pluralism, non—discrimination, tolerance, justice, solidarity and equality between women and men prevail’. it is therefore our duty to act, under the very same treaty that hungary decided to ratify. the civil liberties committee, after consulting four committees of this house, concluded that an article 7.1 procedure is inevitable. i do not think lightly about triggering that article, but if this house fails to use this emergency brake, we fail to deliver to european citizens what was promised to them in the treaty. colleagues, the time has come to make an important choice. will you let a government violate the values upon which this union was built without consequences, or will you ensure that the values of this union are more than just words written on a piece of paper? colleagues, i count on your support. (applause)

rapporteur. – mr president, some of the speakers asked why this report is based on sources from outside of the union; some called it ‘cut and paste’. i will explain why. it’s because within the union there are no reports being written that are benchmarks on fundamental rights and democracy per member state. for instance, the fundamental rights agency in vienna, one of our agencies, is not allowed to do that. and why is that? because member states in the union have been shying away from looking at the state of their country when it comes to democracy, fundamental rights and the rule of law. some speakers asked why hungary? why not romania, slovakia, malta? well, i’ve been here since 2009. if you look at my track record i have been supporting every debate that deals with rule of law in a member state, and i can tell you that romania is going to be on the agenda next time if you all agree with a proposal that the greens have made. and i know that the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs is going to visit malta and slovakia next week to look into the issues around the murder of journalists and corruption. i think that is important. but two wrongs do not make a right, and that’s the mistake a lot of people here are making. if you point to a mistake by somebody else, it doesn’t compensate for the mistakes you make yourself. i think we should keep that in mind. now, if you, as a representative of a minority grouping, manage to break through a glass ceiling, good for you. it doesn’t mean that everybody else in your society is able to follow you through the glass you’ve broken, and it is that possibility that society needs to create because, in the end, what is a government? a government is a service provider to its people – all its people – and a government that is elected with such an enormous majority has extra responsibility to bring along all its people. that is what article 2 is about: people. article 2 is about respect for human rights, including everybody, and article 2 is not about nepotism. nepotism is not a european value. i ask the house to vote in favour of this report. tomorrow commissioner juncker will talk about the state of the union. well, this is a decisive moment for the state of our union.
mr president, our copyright needs updating. but the protection of copyright, and ever more of it, is not the direction that europe needs. this has been a depressing debate with claims of fake news flying back and forth, rock star experts and aggressive lobbying from big companies, on both sides of the argument. but this should not distract us from the substance of what is at stake. so with 50 colleagues we seek a compromise that puts the public interest first and is proportionate between the need to enforce copyright but also to respect fundamental rights. so that means no upload filters, but proportionate remuneration for creators. it means no publishers’ rights, but presumption rights. it means new and clear text and data mining exemptions, which has not been discussed much but which is crucial for the development of europe’s artificial intelligence industry, which is something of great concern as well. we also speak of a freedom of panorama and an exemption for user-generated content. so i hope for your support, so that we can move on together and show compromise can work.

on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, i have to say that i’m a little bit confused having heard all the speakers, including ms trebesius who is now leaving, because apparently the fact that we don’t all have a proper pension later on is to be blamed on either evil neo-capitalists, the european union, the european central bank or immigrants. ms trebesius is not sticking around to explain that to me, because i actually think that we rely a great deal on immigration for our economy and for the future of our health-care and pension systems. never mind. i’m not really sure why we’re having this debate today on ‘fighting privatisation and strengthening public universal social security systems’. i mean it sounds as if a fierce attack were being mounted on our pension systems by private actors. i have not noticed that. i may not have read the newspapers properly but, as far as i can see, in those cases where there is an attack on pension systems, it’s twofold. one type of attack is by trade unions and left-wing parties who are refusing reform of the public systems, at the expense of the younger generation. they’re protecting the rights of the elderly. the average age of the board members of pension funds is, i believe, 56! i think there should be more 18-year-olds on the boards, deciding about their own future. okay. the second type of attack is actually by governments because the attack that i’ve seen in certain countries, mainly in central and eastern europe, has involved the government nationalising pension funds. this is basically doing what is being advocating here. they nationalised the pension funds in what i would call highway robbery, because people’s savings then went into the public coffers and were used for other purposes. in my dictionary that’s theft. in the pan-european personal pension product (pepp) report – because of course that’s at the root of all this – we explicitly emphasised the need for strong first and second pillars. nobody has denied the need for a strong public system, but there is also a third pillar – and i’m coming to a conclusion, madam president! there is a third pillar which may allow people to achieve a proper pension in countries where the state systems do not supply that. that is what we want to achieve. i hope those people will not be denied their rights. (applause)
president. - the next item is the report (doc. it is often a sensitive subject, especially in my own country, and that is not so strange. the european union has very little competence in this area. the member states are responsible for the design of their social and pension systems and this must remain the case. it's the only way. the differences in pension provision between eu member states are huge. it is impossible and undesirable to turn this into a sort of sausage for european unity. pension system deserve to be tailored. and that customization can be delivered at the national level, especially. but that does not mean that we do not share a common challenge. ageing, for example, is happening in all european countries. at the turn of the century, we had about four workers for every pensioner. in a few decades, we will be reduced to two workers who will have to pay the costs of one pensioner. so we are faced with a huge challenge in terms of the affordability of the first pillar in particular. this challenge is in all countries. we must therefore ensure that our legislation does not make it more difficult for those countries which already have successful second pillar pensions, but rather that we encourage other countries to set up similar systems. when we voted on the imps legislation here, we instructed the commission to set up a high-level working group to look at how to stimulate occupational pension provision. i would be very grateful if commissioner oettinger could give a little update on that. at the same time, i regret - as do others - that the left group is actually trying to create a kind of pseudo-opposition between the first pillar and the other pillars in this debate. an honest look at the future shows that if we want good old age provision for all european citizens, we need a healthy mix of the three pillars. the sooner the left realizes this, the better, for all european citizens, both now and in the future.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the elderly are cared for. that's got to be the priority. the search for financial products is often involved. that's very human. we'd rather let money work than work ourselves. again, that's very human, but not always wise. for three reasons: first, the return on money is low at the moment. interest rates are lower than ever. that means money will not work for us and yet there is talk in this parliament of a pan-european pension product. again, people will have to save money, and the money will not bring much. the interest rate is low. the yield is low. what we do need is people who stay fit, who enjoy going to work, and who can do that work, even later in their careers. but to do that, we need to invest, not save. the second reason is that money seeks financial return, whereas it should be about social return. not just profit, but people and planet. that's exactly what sustainable finance is about, which is a broader view so that pension funds and insurance companies don't just put money into fossil fuel companies, companies that violate human rights, or companies that evade taxes. there's also a social return. thirdly, there is no return without risk. so the point is that we should arrive at a fair distribution of those risks. we are not looking at an individualisation of the pension system, but we are looking at a collective insurance of risks, so that everyone can enjoy the benefits of old age and people do not have to fear old age.
president. - thank you to the rapporteurs and colleagues for the excellent cooperation. we're on the right track. this weekend, the plastic cleaner for the ocean cleanup project chose the vast soup of the pacific ocean. i am therefore proud of the initiative taken by boyan slat, a 24-year-old dutch student. he's started and he's understood what policy makers haven't really understood for years. we must act. now is the time to prevent the plastic soup from growing and taking over. the soup has to be cleaned up slowly by projects like ocean cleanup or the fishermen in the north sea, when they're fishing. we should help them too. unfortunately, this waste also includes a number of disposable forks and the like which would be biodegradable under the influence of oxygen but which do not appear to be biodegradable in the aquatic environment. we're being played. this is foam plastic! we must therefore move towards fully reusable plastics and an internal market for secondary raw materials. manufacturers must design products that can be reused. the production of plastic waste is unacceptable because microplastics pollute our environment and our seas. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's environmental policy is not distorted. substances of concern must not be allowed to enter the circular raw materials when reused, as others have said. commissioner katainen, it is time for a clear policy framework and better legislation. you have the efsi funds. let's use it for this, too. we've got plenty of work to do with this. and of course we have to work on the necessary promotion: mothers, children, students, everyone can do something about it. i would like to conclude by saying a few words to my english colleague who has just left. i would like to say: go on a day trip with the english fishermen. i hope they can convince you, because it's almost too late.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0179/97). the oecd has found that in just 30 years, the use of antibiotics in the world has increased by as much as 67%. in human health, antibiotics are still prescribed far too readily. as many as 50% of the antibiotics prescribed are ineffective. in addition, in some parts of the world, large amounts of antibiotics are still being released into the environment through wastewater during the manufacture of medicines. outside the eu, antibiotics are still used in livestock farming as growth promoters to produce cheap meat. that is alarming, and it should be, when you consider that the effectiveness of the antibiotic is rapidly declining and that we are no longer able to treat simple and common diseases and infections. in addition, there has been no research on antibiotics since 1987 and no new classes of antibiotics have been developed. action is needed and countries cannot do it alone, the european union cannot do it alone, it must be tackled globally. i am a strong supporter of a paris agreement on antibiotic resistance. we need to crack down on abuse. we need to do a lot more research into alternatives like vaccination and develop new antibiotics and we need to focus a lot more on preventing disease in humans and animals than we do on curing it. and that can be done, as evidenced by several member states in the european union, where human health care is effectively using antibiotics. or in my country, where farmers have fully invested in new stalls, better hygiene and better management protocols. in seven years, the use of antibiotics in livestock farming has fallen by 60%!
i thank the rapporteur for the good cooperation, but i can say: a very good cooperation. we are facing a big problem, a problem that threatens our very existence. we don't think about it enough. antibiotic-resistant bacteria could become a threat by 2050 that disrupts our entire modern society, like the 2008 financial crisis. when will the european commission and the oecd agree on the actual current mortality statistics of antibiotic resistance? commissioner, you said 25 000, but others say 40 000. what are we talking about? it's not really about numbers. we need to get this thing up and running. parliament has rightly given a thumbs up to the dutch livestock industry, which has reduced the use of antibiotics by two thirds. but we also look at human health care: the reckless and often pointless selling of critical last-line antibiotics for a little flu! the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. antibiotics are still cheaper than a pack of chewing gum or other things in some member states. that's why there are not enough new antibiotics being developed. alongside strong vaccination policies in the member states, europe must support the development of innovative new antibiotics. in the meantime, we are still waiting for a strategy to deal with pharmaceutical pollution. antibiotic-resistant bacteria in surface water pose an uncontrollable threat if we continue at this rate. i shall also be speaking on this in another debate on the water framework directive. europe must now act more forcefully! resistant bacteria don't stick to borders in these times of migration, and we're all dealing with that.
i would like to thank the commission and the rapporteur for their work on the future of mobility. europe is on the move but faces very big challenges. and one of those challenges is road safety. how are we going to make europe's roads safer for all road users? every year 25 000 people die on european roads as a result of road accidents. i repeat: twenty-five thousand! i'm going to the bank. road safety in europe must and can be improved! the mobility of people and goods continues to increase, which is good for our economy and the prosperity of europeans, but it also creates more congested roads and traffic congestion. i am even more concerned about the environmental and climate damage that this is causing. our mobility can only be future-proof if it is also sustainable. the challenge we face cannot be met by individual member states alone. we need a common european approach, one that is committed to innovation with smart forms of mobility. for example, the development of self-driving vehicles. and that can contribute to fewer road accidents, better traffic flow and lower co2 emissions. but intelligent transport systems are also helping us to move more sustainably, safely and efficiently. i am therefore very pleased about european initiatives, including the european its congress. this congress will be organized in 2019 in the netherlands in my own region of brabant, in the cities of eindhoven and helmond. i would literally say: be my guest! because we are shaping the future of mobility together.
i am speaking on behalf of the alde group. mr president, commissioner, whatever we do and whatever agricultural model we use, food production interferes with nature. and it has an environmental impact. it is our task and the task of the industry to minimise this environmental impact. that is why an integrated approach to crop protection is so important. we have to do a lot more to prevent a plague than we do to cure it. how do we do that? first, by breeding, to produce crops that are more resistant to disease. by focusing more on soil quality. with ecological innovations, like the use of insects and microorganisms, but also technological innovations, like precision agriculture, but then as a last resort to ensure that the harvest is not lost. to prevent food waste, chemical means are sometimes needed. what is important to realize is that the admission procedure we have here in europe for the funds is the strictest in the world. what i want to see is that the search for alternatives, for laxatives and biologics goes faster, that the admission system has specialized people for that, and that there is more manpower and more money to go and do this. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. president. - i have three questions to the commission. first of all: the information that's linked to a blockchain is ultimately traceable back to individuals. that makes them vulnerable, not just to hackers but also to multinational corporations like google, microsoft and amazon. the system is inherently uncontrollable. it's in the design. how is the commission going to control this? secondly: according to article 21 of the general data protection regulation, people have an absolute right to be forgotten. with blockchain, the information goes on the internet and can't be reversed. how is the commission going to enforce the gdpr? and thirdly: the computation of blockchain takes a lot of energy. the two largest blockchains in the world, bitcoin and ethereum, now use as much electricity as the whole of austria. that growth is exponential. how does this fit in with the paris climate agreement?

mr president, human rights are among the core values of the eu. that is what article 2 of the treaty on european union says. but in practice, human rights are still often violated, including by companies. it is therefore urgent that we conclude a treaty which obliges companies to respect human rights, even when they operate across borders and thus escape national legislation. we have john ruggie's guidelines, but they are not binding. that is why a global un convention is necessary. the european commission does not seem to realise that it is not a party to this process. it's a un treaty between countries. the best role the commission could play in this regard is to support the member states and encourage them to advocate a binding convention. i would also like to call on the austrian presidency to take its role very seriously.
