mr president, in essence, this debate is about a question: what kind of car will the future consumer prefer? will it be the high co2- and nox—emitting combustion engine vehicle with quite high maintenance costs and very high fuel costs, or a zero—emission car with almost no maintenance costs and very low fuel costs, if any? i think we all know the answer: the good old combustion—engine car that served us so well for 130 years is ready for its well-deserved retirement. that also means that if in europe we want to build tomorrow’s car, we have to develop it much more quickly than we are doing now. market forces as we have seen are not sufficient, so the legislator should help. therefore, highly ambitious co2 targets are essential for the survival of the european car industry, and that we get climate and clean air for free with that is a wonderful side effect.

mr president, there is no debate for economists. minimum wages are good for people with jobs and bad for people without jobs. the insider-outsider effect is a devilish dilemma. however, the free movement of persons has added an extra dimension to this discussion. because full employment in the european union is an illusion, partly because the euro does not work, there are now endless numbers of people available who are disrupting the local labour market by wanting to work for meagre wages in another member state. the european union is not intervening by addressing this problem, but by hijacking the social agenda of the member states and imposing minimum wages. but fundamentally, that is not an answer. after all, the minimum wage in the netherlands is a millionaire's ransom in slovakia. the european social agenda also affects the legal freedom of negotiation of trade unions and employers' organisations. so it must be otherwise, unless it is a european value to distribute poverty equally. because it's not the employers who are causing the lower wages here. lower and falling wages are inherent in the internal market and the free movement of persons. mr. president, you have to work for a living. the labour market must therefore be accessible to local workers and disruption must be combated. i cannot therefore draw any other conclusion than that the free movement of persons must be called into question.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, it is a fine day for me. i remember three years ago when i went to my home in south limburg on a working visit to the police and judicial services. i visited men and women who work every day to ensure our security in the border region. that is not easy, especially in a province like mine where we have more borders with belgium and germany than with our own netherlands. what has always remained with me from that working visit is the frustration that there was about how difficult it is sometimes to freeze or confiscate criminal assets across the border. when a drug dealer is arrested and prosecuted in the netherlands but his money or his ferrari is parked on the other side of the border, it is very difficult to get hold of it. this is not just a problem in the border region. the commissioner has already said that the size of illegal markets in the eu is about eur 110 billion. we're not going to be able to confiscate more than one percent of that. so a lot of it just ends up in the hands of criminals. there's only one way to hurt these criminals, and that's in their wallets. criminals are not guided or stopped by national borders, so neither can police and justice. this new legislation will make it easier in the future to confiscate the money and resources of criminals on the other side of the border. that's very bad news for criminals and very good news for all of us. it also shows that the eu can tackle these kinds of issues, that eu rules can add value in practice and that we are able to translate the concerns of citizens in the border region into this type of policy. that is our main task in this parliament and i am very glad that we have succeeded in doing so today.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, local and regional authorities across the european union are struggling with the challenges of migration. that is not because of the massive influx, because there are not millions of people arriving, it is because national governments have completely failed time and time again to take decisions, to adopt a proper asylum and migration policy. local and regional authorities unlike the council, cannot afford the luxury to fail, to dither, hesitate, postpone, turn a blind eye, play to the gallery, play into the long grass, so local and regional authorities do what government leaders fail to do, they act. local and regional authorities, but also ngos and churches, are taking their responsibility. national governments, instead of thanking those people, are criminalising them and punishing them for doing what any decent human being would do. courageous local and regional politicians get punished, like a mayor in italy who has been put under house arrest for helping refugees. but that is the world on its head. you know what i think colleagues? i think it is the 28 government leaders who should be under house arrest. maybe we should criminalise them for a criminal failure to act at great human cost. i don’t need to speak about all the shameful and despicable situations that we are facing, not just on eu territory but also outside the eu’s borders. i think it is deeply, deeply shameful. we are the richest continent in the world and we pretend that we are not able to welcome these people; refugees but also labour migrants. parliament has done its homework, and what we have done is we have bridged the political differences across the political groups, which is the responsibility of politicians. the commission has done its homework. now i expect the council next week at the jha council to show the same responsibility and finally agree on the asylum package, because that is what it has to do.
the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. apart from the usual challenges, they often have to ensure that large groups of migrants and refugees are received in a decent way. we have to help them do that. the way in which we will ultimately provide that assistance will be discussed in the context of the amf. but we also have to be honest and realistic. the challenge for many local and regional authorities is compounded by the fact that many people have arrived in europe in recent years who are not actually eligible for asylum. these migrants are not fleeing war or persecution, but are seeking a better future. the energy and resources required at the local and regional level to provide for those people ultimately come at the expense of the much-needed energy and resources we need to find protection for genuine refugees. so therein lies a real challenge for the european union. if we really want to reduce the pressure on regional authorities, we must prevent thousands of people without prospects of asylum from falling into the hands of criminal smugglers and risking their lives by trying to get to europe on a dinghy. we must ensure that asylum seekers who have been processed are actually returned to their country of origin. it is absurd that we only succeed in actually implementing a return decision in 30% of cases. many colleagues have pointed out that the statistics show that inflows have decreased and that the crisis is actually over. but let us not forget that there are still hundreds of thousands of asylum procedures in europe at the moment and that the crisis in this respect is far from over. we must eliminate this backlog and ensure that procedures are short, fast and clear. if we succeed in doing so, we can really alleviate the challenge facing local and regional authorities.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, in today’s eu, independent judges are being dismissed, journalists are threatened and even murdered, ngos are harassed and intimidated. in the last two years alone, since we adopted the proposal for the drf pact, two article 7 procedures have been launched: one by parliament and one by the commission. the commission has also launched infringement cases and took a member state to court. the council organised hearings on the rule of law in that same member state, and parliament created the rule of law monitoring group for two other member states, as well as a plenary debate on yet another member state. so a lot is being done – and i have to commend the commissioner for his actions – but it is piecemeal and erratic. it is too little and, too often, too late. we need a pan-european mechanism for democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights in the eu that is comprehensive and not fragmented; evidence based, not politicised; one that monitors all member states annually and is not ad hoc or incident-driven. exactly two years ago in this house, we adopted such a proposal: the drf pact. we proposed a fully—fledged interinstitutional agreement that we could have agreed on today. so why have the commission and the council so far failed to act on our proposal? you work on the rule of law, but your refusal to agree the comprehensive drf pact means that forces that demolish our way of life are always ahead of us. so it’s time to act because, commissioner, i don’t agree with you. the toolkit that we currently have at our disposal is visibly not enough. so, as the commission and member states drag their feet, parliament should move ahead anyway. i propose that we start our own annual drf cycle, including country reporting and a debate on the state of democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights in europe, in tandem with national parliaments, and we don’t wait until the council and commission are ready. because in conclusion, democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights cannot defend themselves. we have to stand up for them or they will perish.
i see mr krasnodębski's plea on germany as a complement, as support for what parliament is asking for, namely not to wait until the calf is drowned, not to drag on for eight years to finally get a two-thirds majority to start an article 7 procedure for hungary, but to look at the state of the rule of law, democracy and fundamental rights in each member state, structured, annually, as a kind of visiting committee, as is done at universities. it is nice that the council takes up a subject each year and then talks to each other, but it is typical that the council still shy away from simply inviting the european parliament to come and explain the report on hungary. because that's all it is. so as long as we have to turn to outside institutions, namely the council of europe, the un, the osce, and our own fundamental rights office, to produce reports like mine on hungary, and do not allow for real country-by-country analyses, we are in effect turning a blind eye to the problems in our own region.
i would also like to thank my colleague carlos coelho, commissioners king and avromopoulos and all the shadow rapporteurs who worked with us on this result, and of course the council, in particular the estonian and bulgarian presidencies. i think we've achieved a very good result. the schengen information system is a crucial part of our security policy. it's a component that actually works very well, but we don't hear much about it. it is crucial that national authorities, police and judicial authorities can access information through this system on, for example, wanted persons, persons to be discreetly followed, persons to be arrested, missing persons and missing children, but also on stolen objects such as cars, passports or weapons. let me give you some figures to tell you what is happening to that system. it's accessed five billion times a year. we've logged over 70 million of these signals into that system. 450 times a day in the eu we have a hit based on this system. we have arrested 40,000 suspects on the basis of this system, 150,000 people have been refused entry at the border, 28,000 missing persons have been found, 200,000 objects have been recovered. these are all very impressive figures, but we can always do better and we must always do better, and that is why we are reforming the schengen information system. carlos coelho has already said a number of things, so let me go into one specific aspect a little more deeply, and that is the inclusion of return decisions in the schengen information system. this is an important step, because we all know that we are having great difficulty in actually returning people who have not been granted asylum in europe and who must actually return to their country of origin. at present, only 37% of cases are successful. that means that in almost two-thirds of the cases, people who came here, who were not granted asylum, stayed. that number, that percentage, must absolutely rise if we are to maintain the support for the common asylum policy. we need to improve this and the schengen information system can help us do that. now, when a member state has decided not to grant asylum and a person has to go back to their country of origin, we offer voluntary return, so to speak, put a tick on it and just believe that it is right. there are two problems here. first of all, we don't know if that person actually left. secondly, if that person turns up in another member state, the authorities there are often unaware that a return decision has already been taken in another country and we start over. we are solving both of those problems today. the moment someone actually crosses the eu's external border, the original member state is informed and corrected so that we can focus our energy on the people who are still here. police services can now see in the system whether someone is already marked as a person who should be returned to the country of origin, so that cooperation is much easier to make that happen in an efficient way. this is an important step towards making the return policy more efficient. at the same time, we must therefore be careful. that is why we in the european parliament have made sure that the safeguards in this area are also being seriously strengthened. the alert shall be deleted when a return decision is suspended or postponed. the principle of non-refoulement and the geneva convention are explicitly included. the safeguards for data protection are being greatly improved, both in terms of data retention and cooperation with third countries in this field. so in summary, mr president, we have succeeded in bringing the return policy one step closer to real efficiency, while ensuring that the safeguards and rights of individuals are respected at all times. that is a very good result and we should be proud of it.

credible on limiting the worst biofuels, palm oil in particular. we will be watching closely.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on agriculture and rural development. president, commissioner, climate scientists around the world are concluding that if we are to avoid the worst effects of climate change, we must not allow the earth to warm by more than 1.5 degrees. but individual countries cannot do this alone, so european cooperation is urgently needed. i am therefore also pleased that the eu now has ambitious plans to save energy and to use renewable energy. but it must not become an end in itself. it is about reducing co2 emissions, and i also believe that we should seriously consider the use of nuclear energy. what i am very pleased about is that the criteria for biofuels have been tightened up in this legislation. because it is not possible that we use biofuels which emit more co2 than, for example, fossil fuels, and that this will lead to a collision in the production of these biofuels.
mr president, there are also sensible issues that are put on the political agenda by the establishment parties: clean energy or plastic waste in the wild or in the sea, we can't really argue with that. i do not think the eu should interfere, but these are sensible issues in themselves. the question is: why now? why all the excitement now? because when we talk about plastic in the ocean, it's been a problem for the last 10 to 20 years. during my time in the national parliament, activist groups and environmental movements came to tell me that. and they've been here, too. there are many members of parliament who are still sitting here, but so are the parties, who knew of the problems, they knew of the seriousness and did nothing. and now you do! and that's because old political parties don't really get moving until the political agenda is dominated by a subject that they don't like. and that, in this case, is migration and islam, the subject of my political movement. this is a very popular subject with the european public and therefore the old political parties are having to go crazy looking for a competing subject. and now they've found it. but that's not intrinsically motivated! if that had been the case, the parties which were already in power in the vast majority of our countries would have done something about it. and they didn't! you can blame them for that, because that has only exacerbated the problem. and you can agree with my political line on migration and islam or you can disagree with it, but it's been intrinsically motivated and consistent for 15 years.
the mediterranean is wide and the mediterranean is deep, but the bottom is still visible. fish and other marine species in the mediterranean are disappearing at an alarming rate, particularly in the adriatic sea, where half of all marine mammals and fish have disappeared in the last 50 years alone. scientists warn that the pressure on the ecosystem is so great that if we do nothing now, the point of no return is imminent. there's no going back if we keep going on this foot. the plant and animal species in the adriatic will be wiped out. i have been closely involved in the north sea and baltic sea fisheries plans for the past few years, and those plans were not very good, but this plan for the adriatic really beats everything. this is a carte blanche for overfishing, a carte blanche to eradicate anchovies and sardines, species which are very important for the adriatic ecosystem. i therefore think that the rapporteurs who drew up this fisheries plan should be ashamed of themselves. they are putting the survival of ecosystems at risk and i hope that parliament will put a stop to this. the adriatic needs a rescue plan, a plan that stops overfishing now and not in three years, now, as agreed in the united nations sustainable development goals. i also believe that european agricultural and fisheries subsidies should be abolished.
the rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on budgetary control. president, thank you to the co-rapporteur, mrs ayala sender, for her contribution. the committee on budgetary control has a historical focus on the legality of expenditure. but fortunately in recent years there has also been an increasing focus on efficiency. the hundreds of billions of euros we spend here must also serve a political purpose. spending the money is not for nothing. it's not about redistributing money. it is about achieving clear political objectives. the results of the research must be clearly defined and the results measurable. i hope that the european commission and the council, and the members of this house, will read the court of auditors' reports carefully, because they say time and again in almost every policy area that there is something wrong with that efficiency. last week there was a very critical report on the common agricultural policy as proposed by the european commission. it's great that we're going green, very much needed. but the way the commission wants to go green is with non-measurable targets, i.e. the money is spent but the environmental objectives will not be met. so a call to the commission on behalf of the committee on budgetary control: i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter.
i am very much in favour of spending more money on those issues, on those challenges that individual member states cannot tackle alone, such as migration, climate, security and innovation. but as with any company, any association and also in the national budget, if you spend more money somewhere, you also have to look at where you can save money and at least be critical that the money is spent effectively. the european parliament is completely wrong here! the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. that doesn't inspire confidence in the voters. it is pretending that there is a european money tree, instead of realizing that the money comes from the taxpayer. that is why i am also a strong supporter of the introduction of the so-called european salmon standard. if you spend more money in one area, you have to cut back in another to balance the budget. the european parliament, however, is doing the exact opposite. the commission is also proposing to introduce new european taxes before approving the new multiannual budget. this is not constructive. first, the european parliament must show that it can handle european taxpayers' money with care.
the majority of the european parliament, mr president, is in favour of an even more absurd and costly multiannual financial framework than the commission has proposed in the past. the netherlands is also ignoring the annual rebate of over 1 billion euros. the budget presented by the commission was already a disappointment, but the proposed amendments make it even worse. the budget is said to contain far too little money to solve all the problems that europe and the world can think of. but that is not the union's task either! what started as economic cooperation has become a multi-headed monster that meddles in everything, at the expense of individual states that are often very well able to solve their own problems. there is no need for brussels bureaucrats. i call on parliament to stop implementing the un's sustainable development goals, to stop pursuing an inclusive europe, to stop the obsession with gender neutrality, to stop the climate hysteria, to stop the islamisation of europe and to stop embracing non-western immigrants. finally, i tabled an amendment to maintain the dutch rebate. the netherlands has been paying billions of euros more than we receive for many years. the fact that the annual rebate is even being considered is ridiculous and unacceptable. this is the time to make adjustments and to cut the budget considerably. the failed eu project has cost far too much for decades. stop this madness!

rapporteur. – madam president, we are going to discuss here a file on regulating co2 standards for heavy—duty vehicles for the first time in european history. i think that it is very important to stress that quite a few regions in the world are already putting co2 standards in place, like the united states, canada, china and japan. they have all put co2 regulation in place on the heavy—duty vehicle sector. europe hasn’t done so yet. but we now have a proposal on the table from the commission for co2 standards for heavy—duty vehicles, and i think that is of course a very welcome development. why? because, if you look at heavy—duty vehicles, this is one of the sectors in europe that is fast increasing and where co2 emissions are also fast increasing. for now, total eu co2 emissions are at a level of 6%, of which 25% is from road transport and it is increasing. we know that to meet our climate targets agreed in the paris agreement we need to go to zero emissions. this absolutely means that special attention needs to be paid to the heavy—duty vehicle sector, which this proposal is doing. for context, what we know is that, if you want to be paris compatible, the heavy—duty vehicle sector needs to go in the direction of minus 40% by 2030. that analysis is from the dutch institute, tno, who say that for compatibility with the paris agreement, you need to go to those kind of reduction levels, and we’re not there yet. that’s also why we as a parliament, in the committee on the environment, public health and food safety (envi), agreed on raising the ambition level compared to what the commission was proposing to a level that we say should be minus 20% by 2025 and minus 35% by 2030. i want to stress that this level of minus 20% by 2025 is, according to the impact assessment from the commission, also possible with existing technologies. we know of course that, if you look to the longer term and we need to go to lower level ambitions, then we need to have an improvement in technologies because existing technologies can get to a level of minus 30%, but not much more than that. this means that we also need to prepare for new technologies in the heavy—duty vehicle sector and that’s why, also in envi, we have been proposing a zero and low—emission vehicle mandate to push the market into new technologies. the definition of zero and low—emission vehicles is broad, so it’s not necessarily electricity – it can also be other technologies. we are not subscribing to any technology, but are pushing just for a mandate on zero and low—emission vehicles of 5% by 2025, rising to 20% by 2030, in order to prepare the market for these new technologies to arrive in order to get to the deeper carbon cuts that are necessary for implementation of the paris agreement. we are also putting forward a specific mandate for buses. i want to stress that this is important because in some of these markets the truck producers also produce buses and, if you have just one mandate and mix them all, you can create an unfair market. therefore it is important to have separate mandates for buses and trucks because we know that on buses we can move much faster we can than on trucks. the mandate is also for all the trucks, not only the bigger ones, so that you can also achieve those zero and low—emission vehicles mandate for smaller trucks. my last point is that there was a big discussion on the fine. let me say very clearly that we would like to stay very close to the commission proposal because, if you lower the fine to such a level that it’s easy for the producer to pay it, what you are putting in place is just an additional price factor – a cost factor of production – and i don’t think that is what we want to do. to conclude, i would like to thank all the shadows for all the good cooperation. on many compromises we had broad support, though not on others. we do of course see some amendments on the targets. that’s to be expected. but i would like to thank all the shadows for their great cooperation. i think the vote in envi, where 47 people were in favour and only six against, shows that we had broad support in that committee, which we hope also to maintain in the plenary. (applause)
madam president, i assume that if you asked most europeans if the co2 emissions of trucks and buses are tackled in the eu they would say ‘yes’. i assume that they would be surprised if we explained to them that that’s not the case up till now. so i’m very happy that we are finally catching up with other parts of the world. that’s not only necessary to keep the paris climate agreement in sight, it’s also necessary, as commissioner arias cañete already emphasised, to safeguard the competitiveness of our own truck manufacturers in europe. let’s not forget all those truck operators, which most of the time are sme companies, that can save tens of thousands of euros if the trucks that they’re using are much more fuel efficient – a very important element as well. finally, i’m a great supporter of a separate and ambitious target for zero—emission buses. our european cities are struggling a lot with air quality. let’s not get the same problem as with zero—emission vehicles, that the clean buses are simply not available if they’re asked for. so please also vote in favour of that part of this great report by our colleague, mr eickhout.
rapporteur. – madam president, i would like to thank the commissioner for his replies and to thank my colleagues for this very good discussion that we have had. i feel that there is quite some support for what we did in the committee on the environment, public health and food safety (envi), which is very good. to touch on a couple of issues that have been raised, first, on the level of ambition, we have to be very clear here that we have already adopted national binding targets for 2030 under effort sharing. this means that we know that if in the transport sector we are not reducing our emissions enough, reductions will need to come from the other sectors that fall under the effort sharing regulation, being housing or agriculture. if we don’t go for the cost—effective reductions that are possible in transport, it will mean that agriculture needs to deliver more in those countries where they will have difficulties in meeting their reduction targets. all the analysis shows that a co2 standard on cars and heavy—duty vehicles is the cost—effective way to reduce emissions in those sectors that fall under the effort sharing regulation, and that’s what we’re doing here. on the level where we, as envi, say that we should raise the bar to 20%, i once again want to stress that, in the commission’s impact assessment, it’s very clearly stated that an analysis of the cost—effective technologies already at hand shows that full deployment would make it possible to achieve emission reductions between 15% to 20% by 2025. this shows that the existing technologies can be achieved with the 20% target. on top of that, we need to develop new technologies and that’s why we are making this regulation consistent with the cars. in the cars we had super credits and now, later on, the commission is stepping away from that in cars and comes up with super credits again in this regulation. we think it’s better to skip that mistake that you made on cars and immediately go into a mandate for zero and low-emission vehicles at a very low level of 5%. my last point, on buses, is that it’s very important to realise that there are producers producing both trucks and buses. so if we have one mandate, you are giving very clear priority and a benefit for those who are producing both because, in buses, we can move faster, and the market is moving faster. that’s why we have decided on separate mandates for buses and heavy—duty vehicles – in order to make sure that the level playing field remains equal for all the players in the market for new technologies. once again, i thank everyone for the discussion. i’m looking forward to the vote and then indeed, what is the intention? a fast resolution in negotiations, hopefully to start under the austrian presidency.
rapporteur. – mr president, thank you very much. i will not make a long speech; i will only say according to the fourth subparagraph of rule 59(4) that the plenary gives a mandate for direct negotiations with the council.
the european parliament has been a great help to the people of europe in the past, and it is a great help to the people of europe today. "all that is of value is defenseless". if we do not act, lucebert seems to be right, and so we must now ensure the resilience of our democracy by acting against foreign influence. that's a difficult balance to strike. i have just listened to the last three speakers who are actually saying in the context of media freedom: let the russians pull the strings here. the eu is quickly accused of propaganda. but doing nothing is not an option! i will give you the concrete examples, especially for the profession behind me. let me start in my own member state, the netherlands. the day after mh17 was shot down, putin's troll factories went to work three times as hard to shift the blame via social media towards ukraine. then there is brexit. there are also findings that kremlin trolls wanted to influence the referendum. worse, we already have a ruling party in our own european union that uses manipulated video footage on social media. i welcome the proposals of the european commission. the fight against this is very necessary and must be fought on several fronts. first of all, of course, to make people aware of fake news through education and through political awareness campaigns, in close consultation with the member states. that's where it all starts. we need more transparency. this can be done in consultation with tech companies, but also through legislation if those companies do not come across the bridge. it's about transparency about funding, about where it's coming from, about who's behind political ads and about algorithms on social media platforms. actually, we have to think a step further. because what about developments like deepface, for example, where video footage is manipulated in such a way that politicians are put words in their mouths that they didn't say themselves. in fact, truth or development has already caught up with us and we may have to do more, dear commissioners, than is already on the table. we support you in that.
mr president, a person’s right to vote by secret ballot and universal suffrage for his or her own government representation is enshrined in the universal declaration of human rights and this very right is now being challenged by foreign actors – and russia has been mentioned but it is not alone. many are using misinformation as a form of hybrid warfare or even more sophisticated techniques, such as deep fakes, through ai and machine learning, to deceive. they’re meant to erode the trust in the public debate and in liberal democracy itself. so there’s a lot at stake and we must do what we can. what i missed in the introduction by the commissioner, which i generally thought was very comprehensive, is a notion of an assessment of the very business models of facebook, youtube and others, that are designed to sell more advertisements. whether or not that lists conspiracy theories on the top, it doesn’t really matter for them, they are not developed to advance democracy, but i do think they should respect the rule of law. in that context self—regulation is not enough. i think every chance has been taken, and we’ve seen that it’s not enough, so responsibility has to be taken, algorithmic accountability is needed, a sector inquiry is needed and we need to look at bots. bots don’t have freedom of expression the way individuals do. we need transparency of politically—funded advertisements and in the use of micro—targeting. we need stress tests of our election technology, because the weakest link can hurt all of us. and political parties and candidates needs to be prepared, and so should journalists, in how they may or may not deal with leaks. in other words, we need accountability. the survival of liberal democracy is at stake.
i remembered the cartoon popeye, the sailor who only after a can of spinach was strong enough to protect his girlfriend. just as popeye had to protect olive against the rogue pluto, so we must protect our european democracy against rogue foreign actors. we saw it in the elections in the united states. we saw it in the brexit referendum. we saw it in the netherlands in the investigation into the downing of mh17, which left nearly 300 people dead. our job is to make sure that this list of examples stops here, that this undermining of our democracy stops. with the european elections coming up, it is certainly an important step towards strengthening our resilience in that area much further. the creation of the stratcom task force was and is a good idea in principle, but let us do it well. it wasn't until january of this year that money was put into this team: the amount of the loan is eur 1.1 million. by comparison: the russians have already spent over four times that on a brexit campaign alone! we cannot afford to be complacent and to express our concerns, especially as the european elections are approaching. just as popeye needs spinach to defeat pluto, so we in the european union need powerful weapons to stop this kind of foreign interference. let us at least make sure that those weapons are delivered from this house.
interference in an election. i could be talking about putin's troll army or the italian deputy prime minister who calls journalists jackals. but i want to talk about american interference. facebook has insisted for years that it is neutral. but with all that neutrality, it has allowed fake news to be fabricated in a small town in macedonia, dark ads to affect filter bubbles, and cambridge analytica to get away with 50 million profiles. this is how facebook has contributed to trump and brexit. in 2016, about 1.4 billion euros was spent on online campaigning, which facebook and zuckerber are making money from. zuckerberg is promising to make things better. but it has to be faster and it has to be better. i would like to see a compelling european approach, full disclosure of spending for a political purpose by facebook (who, what, where, when) and finally also an ex ante test on the algorithms, on the formation of public opinion. because let's not let facebook impose its will on our democracy, but the other way around.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection. a big thank you to the rapporteur of the committee on transport and tourism for the good cooperation and of course thanks to the many imco members and shadow rapporteurs who contributed to the original opinion of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection, which they were able to accept almost unanimously in the end. that's a good signal for the passengers. i think the end result is very good, especially in terms of compensation. i do not agree with the commission that this would be outside the scope of the recast. we have clear justifications for this. however, you cannot explain to passengers that they will only be reimbursed half the ticket price if they are more than two hours late. that is something that should be addressed in this initiative. there's good news for the disabled. they can count on assistance quickly and do not need to give days' notice of their arrival at a station. the bikes were reported, that was important too. there's really only one point left. let us not, please, allow railways to say that it was bad weather and that you lose your rights as a passenger. we've seen too many leaves on the tracks, even in the netherlands, as an excuse. we shouldn't want that. we shall have to decide that tomorrow at the vote.
traveling by train is a sustainable alternative to the polluting car or the kerosene-sniffing airplane. if we encourage more people to take the train more often, we will contribute to making our mobility more sustainable. we will then contribute to the achievement of the paris climate targets. but then we have to make rail travel across europe a lot more attractive. this proposal will strengthen the rights of rail passengers throughout europe and i think that is a step in the right direction. the new rules will allow train passengers to receive up to 100% of the ticket price in the event of delays anywhere in europe. and that's a big difference from the situation today. now only 50% of the ticket price can be recovered. a second point that has already been mentioned: the passengers can more easily take their bicycles on board the train. in a bicycle country like the netherlands, this is of great importance. we will also ensure that transport to the stations and the journey to the destination is sustainable. the third point is that it will be much easier for passengers with reduced mobility, for people with disabilities, to take the train in europe. they can now count on assistance on the platform without having to arrange it days in advance. it'll make their travel arrangements much easier. ladies and gentlemen, let us vote tomorrow on the proposal to make trains more attractive in europe. not only will european rail passengers be grateful to you for this, but so will the climate!

on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, the joint comprehensive plan of action (jcpoa), or the nuclear deal that was agreed by the major global powers at a critical time, was actually fatally damaged by president trump’s withdrawal. but that doesn’t mean that there’s nothing we can do and that all is lost. a reduced nuclear agreement may still work, but i think we, in europe, have to be realistic. of course, high representatives ashton and mogherini stuck out their necks for diplomacy, and that was the right thing to do, but preserving that legacy cannot be a goal in and of itself because it also had a shadow side. i think human rights and the violations that we see every day in iran, as well as iran’s troubling role in the middle east, were overlooked, and rebalancing our european approach towards iran is very important and should not be hindered by needing to defend the jcpoa. attempts to maintain policy independence and to give european companies maximum choice to do business are the right steps to take; they are positive. the eu lifted sanctions as it’s part of the deal, while iran commits to verification of nuclear enrichment. but to push or even punish european companies for choosing to drop business with iran under threat of us repercussions is taking it too far. additionally, the whole impression that the eu and iran are in one sort of camp and that the united states is in another camp is deplorable. we are both liberal democracies, and that should be leading. i think the way forward for us in europe is to broaden our iran policy and to include, very ambitiously, human rights and foreign policy concerns while continuing to take reasonable steps to stay committed to our part of the nuclear agreement.
in writing. although i hoped it would not be necessary in the meantime, i will mention it again: we need to close the pay gap. on average, women earn 16% less than men. although progress has been made in the past, recent research shows that the gap has not narrowed for two years. meanwhile, women are at increased risk of poverty, especially single-parent families and pensioners. this is unacceptable and inexplicable, if you ask me. gender equality in the workplace has not yet been achieved. leave for the birth of a child should not be a restriction on a career, but should be a right for everyone. unpaid work such as housekeeping or care should not force people to work part-time. let's give everyone the opportunity to find their own work-life balance without gender affecting it. equality is not a matter of chance, it is time for action and there is much to be done. the extension of paternity leave is one example. providing good and affordable health care services is also important. these include child care, elderly care, and facilities for the disabled or chronically ill. the solutions are ready: let's close the hole once and for all.
the increase in lyme disease is a matter of great concern and a major problem. there is no european consensus on treatment, and diagnosis and screening of lyme disease is not discussed, and national practices vary considerably. unnecessarily many patients do not have access to the treatment that is appropriate for them or do not receive the correct diagnosis right away. i also speak from personal experience from our own family: my cousin just had the effects of lyme disease and its bacterial infection last summer. he went to the gp. the family went to the doctor, and the doctor just says to this kid, you just have the flu, go home. at the last moment, he almost died. it's all right. but these consequences are also being discussed in austria and we must work together on this immediately. because even the gps don't know what's going on. i therefore welcome the european commission's implementing decision to include lyme borreliosis in the list of communicable diseases. let us now all recognize this terrible disease as an occupational disease and promote research and awareness raising to prevent it. but not only that, the whole family, our whole neighborhood was wondering: what are we gonna do about it? it's not just people working in the wild. they're kids who ride bikes, even from cities. it's the civilians too! it just requires a national approach and thank you for your efforts.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, i would like to thank the commission for this strategy. i quote mr šefčovič, ‘our strategy now shows that by 2050 it’s realistic to make europe both climate neutral and prosperous, while leaving no european and no region behind’ – it’s beautiful – and mr arias cañete, ‘going climate neutral is necessary, possible and in europe’s interest’. it’s a beautiful press release, so i was eager to read it. i’m a bit confused now because, at the end of the document, you conclude, ‘well, you know, the eu should adopt and submit an ambitious strategy by early 2020 to the united nations framework convention on climate change (unfccc), as requested under the paris agreement’, so our mid—century strategy for this beautiful scenario will be published sometime in early 2020. now i’m really confused. i thought we were doing that here. this is just eight scenarios on the table and, more importantly, it’s avoiding one of the key questions: what are we going to do by 2030? there is a gap in reaching the paris commitments. this document is beautiful, but this document is avoiding the question ‘what is europe going to do by 2030?’ that’s the question in katowice. that’s our economic and our environmental challenge, and the commission is just postponing, as always. unfortunately, i can only conclude that, despite the nice words, climate change policy is not a priority for this commission.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 2052/88 on the common organization of the market in milk and milk products (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). we hear different stories from eyewitnesses. so i went with my colleagues in this parliament to look for myself and we found something quite different. in croatia, for example, i saw this image: a bull is tackled on one leg on a cargo ship. the animal was exhausted and jumped overboard in panic. the seriously injured animal was then transported on board to lebanon. it's unclear if it ever made it there alive. these are not incidents. this is the daily practice. and it's still going up. romania announced last week that it would immediately transport 200 000 sheep to the middle east on a ship which has been banned in australia from transporting animals because of repeated and serious animal suffering on board. i therefore ask for your support for an urgent debate with commissioner andriukaitis on thursday afternoon, because we cannot allow this animal suffering in europe any longer.
rapporteur. – mr president, thank you very much. we don’t have a lot of silence yet, but let me start by thanking all shadow rapporteurs for their wonderful cooperation; we had very smooth negotiations in order to find compromises. so let me start by thanking them for that. secondly, i would like to thank the european commission for its strong proposal for the prolongment of the life financial instrument, which is – let’s face it – the most successful financial instrument that we have in europe. it is very effective, it’s very efficient, and it’s extremely relevant. it’s loved in brussels, but also in the member states by industry and by civil society. i think it’s an example of how european financial instruments should work. it plays a wonderful catalytic role to initiate, expand and accelerate sustainability, and its main success factor is that it’s quality—driven. it is not spoilt by politics. that sounds a bit strange in this political arena, but it’s quality—driven. as far as i’m concerned, as rapporteur – and i found quite some support for that among the shadow rapporteurs – we should continue to keep it quality—driven. of course, even a successful programme can be improved, and there are two elements that i would like to highlight here. first of all, the original fund is far too small: we have eur 5 billion. that’s why this parliament already, in an earlier plenary resolution, asked for an increase to eur 7 billion – very important. the second point is that it should have more coherence with the other eu funds. life is very successful, but too many other european funds are going against the interests of biodiversity, nature and the environment, and especially the strategic integrated projects under the life programme play a very important role here. i would like to highlight two more political elements that were also discussed in this house. the first is that this life budget can only be relatively small if all other eu budget funds are not going against the interests of the environment. that is a very crucial point, also made in all the impact assessments by the commission and in the introduction to the life programme by the commission. it can only have this limited amount of money if the other eu funds are not going against it, and it’s very important that we monitor that after the mff has been improved at large. the second, more political, debate is about national allocations. i’m very happy that we found a compromise among the shadows and that we did not fall into the trap of moving towards national allocations, but that we found a compromise on a bio-geographic balance, which means that we take the ecosystems in europe as a basis and not our national borders. finally, i would like to make some comments on amendments that have been tabled here in plenary. there is one epp amendment that explicitly mentions the agricultural sector and forestry to include them even more strongly in the life fund. i would like to emphasise that, under the current life, agricultural projects are very much eligible. we currently have 136 projects ongoing, and so it’s not only superfluous; it’s also behaving a bit like the very hungry caterpillar. there are agricultural funds of eur 400 billion already reserved, and this limited fund of eur 5 billion should not be more strongly focused on the agricultural sector than it already is. finally, regarding the other amendment from the epp on large carnivores: i know it’s a political debate and it’s worthwhile having that debate here, but putting it among the general objectives of the life programme, which talks about climate, environment and biodiversity in general terms, and then adding large carnivores as equal to those general objectives doesn’t make a lot of sense to me. let’s have that debate when we discuss the other, more individual, parts of it.
the commission is also proposing that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the cap. this week, for example, i'm working on legislation to address unfair trade practices. what we are doing a lot of is making sure that legislation for farmers, which comes mainly from brussels, is workable and that farmers can get away with it. what i'm working on, in particular, is that we can start using more animal manure instead of fertilizer. but one thing we seem to forget, and that is something that has been worrying me a lot in recent years, is the appreciation of the farmer and the gardener. because if you open the newspapers now, the farmer is very often used as a scapegoat for climate change, biodiversity loss, environmental impact and you name it. but we seem to forget what farmers have done, for example, and we take it for granted that they are doing nothing. but here are the numbers. the carbon footprint of milk has fallen by 32%, of pork by 35%, of poultry by 46%, of eggs by 38%, of veal by 50%, of sugar by 41% and of greenhouse farming by 59% since 1990. in the last three years 15 000 hectares of agricultural land have disappeared in the netherlands. in the same period, 11 000 hectares of nature have been added. the leaching of fertilisers has fallen dramatically in the last 20 years and many hundreds of plant protection products have been banned, some of which have no alternative. whatever farming system you use - whether conventional or organic - in producing food, you always have an environmental impact and you always interfere with nature. we need to make sure that impact is minimal. 80% of farmers in the netherlands say they would like to achieve this. now we can do two things. one, to see the farmer as a scapegoat and not have any respect for him or two, to see him as an important partner in making agriculture more sustainable. i am glad that in this new legislation for the life programme, which provides money for climate and the environment, they want to take up that glove and want to work with farmers for an even more sustainable agriculture and horticulture.

rapporteur. – madam president, many thanks for that – and thank you, commissioner, for your warm words for the life programme. i’ve learned a lot during the debate, and i think the most important element that i’ve learned is that the issues we disagree on are details in the overall life programme. in general, we agree. we agree on quite a big increase of the budget, and we fully agree about the huge importance of this financial instrument. i sincerely hope that that will be reflected in tomorrow’s vote, and that we can start soon after tomorrow’s vote with the negotiations with the council to finalise the whole process before the european elections. one final word: i emphasised in my opening remarks that the life fund is only a small part of the total european budget. it is very crucial. many colleagues have embraced the objectives of life, and it’s important that these objectives are also reflected in all the other funds. this is just eur 5 billion, hopefully eur 7 billion. the other funds altogether are more than 1000 billion. so please take your thoughts about the importance of the environment, about climate policy and about biodiversity when you consider the other 95% of the eu budget.
mr president, this week we have the opportunity to better protect thousands, many thousands of car mechanics, road builders and construction workers from carcinogenic diesel fumes and other carcinogens in the workplace. workers have the right to be well protected and not to be made ill by their work. fortunately, this is reflected in the legislation. if we vote in favour, we can prevent people who work with diesel-powered machinery from getting heart disease, lung problems or cancer from work. if we vote for it, we will prevent so much misery in so many families in europe. we can put an end to this misery with this legislation. i think that is a great compliment to the rapporteur, to the shadow rapporteurs, but also to the commission and the council, for having managed to conclude this deal, which allows more people to do their job with more pleasure and without harm to their health.
the food industry in europe is going from scandal to scandal. think of the horse meat scandal or think of the fipronil in our eggs. one of the biggest scandals of this century is the re-approval of the highly damaging agricultural poison glyphosate. the monsanto papers showed in detail the length of the industry's arm. monsanto paid scientists large sums of money to prove the supposed safety of glyphosate. the european institutions have taken that for granted when they are the very ones responsible for ensuring the safety of our food. concerned citizens were able to gather over one million signatures for the citizens' initiative "ban glyphosate!" well, the european commission did not prohibit it, but it did come up with a proposal to make the whole decision-making process more transparent and independent in the future. according to the party for the animals, the only responsible guideline is the precautionary principle. the health of humans and animals and the right to a healthy environment must always take precedence over the commercial interests of the poison producers. unfortunately, the rapporteur is taking the side of those producers. fortunately, the committee on the environment and the committee on pesticides have taken a different line. they want, as we do, to prevent industry from taking decisions in europe. citizens and independent scientists should have early access to all information and, of course, they should also know who is the source of that information. tomorrow parliament will have the opportunity to show once again that it will not give in to the power of money, that it will not give in to the power of industry. i call on you to choose transparency, reliability and independence. i also believe that european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.
mr president, looking at the story before us, i think we have to think about trust among the citizens, but also trust among the business community. i would therefore like to thank our rapporteur, renate sommer, for her report on this subject. the commission has also found that the risk of a possible breach of the undertaking's obligations to comply with the undertaking's obligations is not excluded. this demonstrated the need for coordinated european risk communication and the fight against fake news and hate speech, while ensuring that our food is safer than ever before thanks to efsa. family farms in the poultry-raising sector are still suffering from the consequences of this and we must find a solution. trust also requires transparency. the commission's proposals do not offer a solution, but unfortunately create new, enormous problems. i share mrs sommer's condemnation of the complete lack of impact assessment, particularly as the rules in this proposal will cause considerable damage to the innovation and competitiveness of european industry. it is incomprehensible that the commission has not carried out an impact assessment. the european food, feed packaging, feed industry, plant protection products, plant breeding and flavouring industries are at risk of being banned in europe. we have to think about that too. i am against the disclosure of details of production processes and associated research studies prior to and at the time of application. this poses a risk to industry because of the corporate espionage from third countries. transparency may also be provided after completion at the time of the efsa's scientific opinion on the draft. i think we should fight each other for that.
president. - i am sorry, mr president, but i have to say that i am not in a position to give you an answer to that question. we even drink carcinogens, such as caffeine and alcohol. in agriculture, conventional and organic farmers use means to protect their crops. that makes sense because if a harvest fails, they have no income and we have no food. it is of the utmost importance that we have a scientific risk assessment to determine whether these products are dangerous to the environment, to humans or to animals. because let 's be honest . how many of you in this room know the chemical formula of glyphosate? no one, so we'll leave it to science, too. what you see very often is that a scientific assessment doesn't quite fit in with our political alley and we're screaming murder and arson. i always compare it a little bit to the situation where we're sitting at home on the bench watching a football game where the referee sees a foul in a tackle by our favorite player. we wish that referee all the best. but it's important that the referee is there, because without a referee, there's no football game. that is why it is so important that we have a trustworthy risk assessment, that we do everything we can to strengthen the authority and that we also do everything we can to strengthen the transparency. because if we do not have an arbiter to judge certain substances, we will be back to a policy of welfare and non-welfare and we will kill all innovation.

the report is the conclusion of our special committee on terrorism, but it is also, and above all, a starting point for continuing our work on the security of 500 million europeans. let me make a few brief remarks. we must make better use of the data we are already collecting, and i share commissioner king's hope that the interoperability dossier will be completed as soon as possible in this respect. we also need to do a better job of storing data. it is ridiculous that four and a half years after the court's ruling, we still have no viable alternative to ensure that the police and the judiciary have access to the information they need. preachers of hate, spreaders of poison in our society, we need to blacklist them so that we make their work impossible. and finally, we need a european system to track financial flows related to terrorism. we are far too dependent on our american colleagues. we will have to take our own responsibility and we will have to take that responsibility quickly. in other words, mr president, there is still a lot of work to be done, so let us get to work.
madam president, japan and the eu are partners in respecting the rule of law, democracy, rules-based trade, multilateralism and openness. this makes the economic partnership agreement and the strategic partnership agreement all the more important in times when strongman politics from washington to beijing are challenging the very model that has brought our citizens the highest quality of life. this is a crucial moment for europe to lead. does this trade agreement solve all our problems? no, but it will facilitate the flow of goods and services between us in a smart and sustainable way, and it also allows us to work towards setting high standards in the digital economy, towards wto reform, towards better environmental protection and reaching the 2030 sustainable development goals. i don’t have to remind you of what is at stake in terms of the threats we face to the rules—based order. to colleagues who do not believe this agreement is perfect, i say that complex agreements never are. but do not let the perfect be the enemy of the good. i look forward to improving our already good relationship with our japanese friends.
on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, opposition leader selahattin demirtaş is facing a maximum sentence of 183 years. he is jailed, his freedom of expression is curtailed, and so is his right to politics. and for what? for representing millions of people in turkey who want peace, millions of people who want to continue their struggle for equal rights through democratic means, and for being the voice of kurdish – and turkish – youth who are fed up with the decades-long bloodshed. the european court of human rights last month unanimously ruled for his immediate release. to say it in the words of the court: ‘it is beyond reasonable doubt that the demirtaş detention had pursued the predominant ulterior purpose of stifling pluralism and limiting freedom of political debate.’ this is the definition of a political prisoner. this is the reason why president erdoğan has no plans to release his political opponent. it’s easier to rule without an opposition, without free press, and without civil society. dear başak, when you are in the court tomorrow to testify in the show trial against your husband, know that you have the european parliament behind you. we will not forget him, and we will continue to demand justice. selahattin, we are with you.
president, human rights: the human rights situation in the it is the common thread of our international action and it is important that it remains so. it is particularly important in today's world that the eu stands up for human rights. human rights violations are by definition never domestic affairs. there's some good news. after years of pressure from the european parliament and others, there seems to be finally some light on the matter of a european sanctions mechanism for human rights violations, with sanctions targeted at individuals involved in human rights violations. the sanctions are not intended to punish a population but to punish government officials or politicians who commit crimes. i welcome this dutch initiative for a european magnitsky act, and i am also glad that the council is supporting it. but let me tell you something: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to take them up.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party president, i too would like to express my horror at the attack that has just been carried out at the christmas market here in strasbourg, and i wish the victims every success. i hope the police will lock up the perpetrators soon and that they can stay there for a very long time. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover the whole of the eu. the amounts in the 2018 budget are lower than the proposed 2019 budget. oettinger's nightmare scenario seems to me to be a good plan. every tenth of a percent budget cut already saves the taxpayer hundreds of millions of euros. the word 'austerity' and the expression 'simplifying' are not in the dictionary of the euro-lovers. that's why i'm proposing the next cut. withdrawing 400 000 erasmus+ grants for schools, professors and students will prevent further eu indoctrination, saving €3 billion a year. the 100 000 volunteers selected for the european solidarity corps will pay for their holidays themselves, leaving us with a surplus of over 300 million euros a year. reduced funding for the european solidarity fund and the european globalisation adjustment fund is leading countries to solve their own problems instead of always putting their hands up. no more billions of euros to the refugee facility in turkey. the court of auditors found that in half of the cases this unnecessary aid did not produce the desired results. the eu always wants more and more. as far as we're concerned, less, much less, is more sensible. the level of the 2018 budget is already high enough. as for us, i call on colleagues to vote against the amended 2019 budget.

tackling climate change is at the top of our european agenda. this can only be achieved if it goes hand in hand with the fight against poverty and social exclusion. it is important that the sustainable transition happens in a social way, so that people with smaller pockets do not unilaterally pay the bill for our climate ambitions. i accept the price tag attached to measures to combat climate change, but i do not accept the unfair burden sharing. many people are willing to do their part to make a sustainable transition, but not if it has to be paid for with higher taxes on everyday groceries, higher energy bills for people in rental housing and subsidies for solar panels for people with a detached commercial property. that's bad for the foundation for the energy transition. we are calling for a european transition fund to help vulnerable regions to bear the social costs of climate change. this is the only way to ensure a successful green and fair transition in europe. sustainable development must become a social project, of which compensation for the poor is a part. we must leave this world healthy for future generations. at the same time, we also have a responsibility to improve the lives of those who are now paying the bill.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it's a slap in the face to people who want us to contribute to schools for children, care for the elderly, safety in the neighborhood. the proposal to tax certain turnover of tech giants is a temporary but necessary measure. sometimes it's the biggest companies in the world that pay the least tax: amazon, google, facebook, but also uber and airbnb. in addition to the problems they cause, such as the spread of fake news, they also fail to pay taxes, to contribute to the collective benefits of and for everyone. at the same time, public support for change is overwhelming. 720,000 people have signed the avaaz petition supporting the digitax proposal. more than 80% of the people in countries where governments oppose the proposal, such as denmark, germany, the netherlands and sweden, say they support it. the same over 80% say we should not wait for an international agreement. we must not give in to american threats. we should not limit the scope of taxation, but rather expand it with netflix and similar providers of digital content. that is exactly what the econ committee is proposing. netflix also has to pay taxes on its digital revenue. in fact, i would like to make a plea for raising the rate from 3 to 5%. only then can there be a level playing field between the digital company and the traditional large company. because it's the digital giants who can pretend not to be in a country to avoid taxes. so raise the rate. the intervention is therefore necessary, public support is high, and yet european countries are hesitating and refusing to reach an agreement. it's not understandable. in a mutual agreement, france and germany threaten to scrap the proposal. it's not understandable. liberals and conservatives want a low rate. it's not understandable. the tax on turnover from digital services is temporary. i told you that already. it must be a prelude to a structural solution. a definition of digital presence, so that digital companies pay their taxes wherever they operate. the european parliament has already called for this in its reports on the common and consolidated corporate taxation. the report by dariusz rosati is welcome and good, because it confirms that. we must realise that the current systems of corporate taxation are totally outdated and are not suitable in an era of globalisation and digitalisation. in conclusion: the road to international agreement. there is a very skewed situation. right now, google pays 86% of its corporate income tax in the united states and only 14% elsewhere. we therefore cannot count on the united states to help us to redress this crooked situation. we'll have to do that ourselves. it is precisely a european initiative that is needed to ensure that the american negotiators come to the table. that is precisely what is needed to ensure that the talks at the oecd actually have a chance of success. this is necessary to prevent each european country from going its own way and introducing a digitax itself. the plans are already there, but they will only fragment the labour market. in short, a digital tax is necessary for basic fairness, a level playing field between companies. it can't wait. it's now, not never.
rapporteur. – mr president, yes, there have been missed opportunities for those that say that we should wait for an international solution. remember that, in 2015, the oecd started a discussion on taxing the digital economy, and it was blocked by the united states. we need a european initiative to bring the americans to the table and discuss taxing the digital economy. to those that say this is not a good tax measure – yes, i am very grateful to othmar karas and alain lamassoure – we have a structural solution that has been put forward by the european commission, that has been supported by the european parliament: a common corporate consolidated tax base, which allows countries to determine where companies make their profits and where they pay taxes. so this is the only thing we are left to the table to bring forward tax justice, not in 2020 or 2021– no, in 2019. so that we have tax justice in which everyone pays their fair share, everyone pays – also the multinationals, also the millionaires, and most certainly the tech giants like google and facebook. let’s give a clear signal from the european parliament and support an ambitious digital service tax.
president. - mr president, we often talk about money in this house, about the european union's money, about the european union's financial interests. these discussions affect people, because the money of the european union is, of course, taxpayers' money. whether it comes to the european union through vat or through national contributions, citizens in the european union all contribute to the european budget. often, they are people who are struggling to make ends meet. this is why reports of conflicts of interest or fraud are particularly acute. we are rightly seeking to impose stricter requirements on the respect for the rule of law in the spending of european funds. we want the citizens to be able to trust that their money is being spent well. in the case of the czech republic, irregularities have been detected in the past in relation to the stork's nest case. and now there are again indications that there may be a conflict of interest and that the czech prime minister is personally benefiting from eu funds. nobody is guilty until proven guilty, but we must be able to count on independent and thorough investigations both in the czech republic itself and through olaf. we have the right to have parliament properly informed. i call on the commission and the council to do so. finally, a general appeal to the commission. the commission has also been asked to provide information on the implementation of the eu anti-corruption strategy. parliament has repeatedly called for this and this issue once again shows that this practice must be resumed. i'll ask the commissioner to take it up again.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. especially when it comes to space travel. and with this eu space programme, we are leading the world. we're proud of that. this is not about dreamy mars missions, but about real progress that benefits the hardworking european here and now. for example, in my own country, the netherlands, we know precisely where and how the ground is falling, thanks to space travel, so that we can maintain our dikes and our infrastructure properly. it's helping us to innovate agriculture even further, to address the climate problem, and to make the best use of space in military operations. but there are concerns. all data produced must be safe and used only for good purposes. our galileo and our copernicus programmes provide a lot of data at a detailed level and we cannot risk that the impact or application of data will lead to damage to our societies. with this package, we're going to invest money, but we're also going to standardize data and open it up for our companies. we are giving start-ups and our smes more opportunities to develop more rapidly. and that means not only better space technologies, but that openness allows us to innovate further everywhere. so this means making agriculture more environmentally friendly with new technologies, making the transport sector cleaner by further optimising transport flows, and also our medical sector can make people better faster. the frontrunners have the best chance of doing so, so with this package we are going to take start-ups and smes in europe one step further. in short: "the sky is not the limit".

and advanced digital skills, and i think our message is quite simple: we need to reinforce our capacity in these fields. we need to reinforce our capacity and ensure that it is widely used in our single market, and the programme we’re discussing today has to make sure we get the much-needed investment to match the ambitions of that strategy. it’s already important. if we want the eu to play a role in the digital transformations of the future we need to act today and we need to invest today. already the demand for new technology is growing at a much faster pace in the eu than the supply. in high-performance computing we see that the lack of adequate resources actually pushes scientists and researchers out of europe. we have an excellent artificial intelligence community, but it is still underdeveloped compared to other markets. fragmentation and relatively low public investment in cybersecurity put our society and economy at risk while the european cyber industry remains very fragmented. from the perspective of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs (libe), this last point especially is very important. cybersecurity is critical to both our prosperity and our security. as our daily lives become more and more digital, they also become more and more exposed, and the risks in recent years have grown exponentially. increasing the trust of citizens and businesses in digital communication, in cybersecurity, is an absolute priority for the eu and i’m very happy that with the report that we will vote on tomorrow, with this programme from our commissioner, this is exactly what we will do. thank you all and we look forward to voting on this tomorrow.

i would like to say, mr president, that i understand and appreciate the commitment and the commitment of mrs meissner. there are probably few subjects on which there is so much agreement in this parliament. of course, the tens of millions of people in the european union who deal with the consequences of a rare disease every day deserve the very best care. of course, everyone should be able to receive a correct and timely diagnosis. and of course, everyone should have access to affordable medicine. no one will disagree with that, including me. but, mr president, unfortunately that is not the way it works. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. it is up to the dutch to determine how health care is shaped in the netherlands. whether a particular medicine is covered by a health insurance company is a matter for the national parliament. the level of the care premium, the content of the care for the elderly, in short the whole care system is a national competence and must remain so. there are 28 member states with 28 different health care systems. it is a bad idea to try to integrate these systems into something that should eventually lead to one big european health care system. because, mr president, one great european health care system is the ultimate aim of the many euro-philists here in parliament. and that must be prevented at all costs. europe is made up of different nations, different peoples. each country, each people has its own traditions and characteristics. it is absurd to think that there can be one model that applies to all member states. care is custom. it is therefore up to the member states. mr president, it is of course a good idea to cooperate internationally. not from the ivory towers of brussels and strasbourg, but from the member states. of course we encourage cooperation, for example between academic hospitals, scientists and the world health organization.
